#2852. Nvidia stock plunges 14%, wraps up longest weekly losing streak since 2008 financial crisis

The last time Wall Street was this down on Nvidia the chipmaker's flagship product was the GeForce graphics processor for high-end PC video games, and markets were reeling from the global financial crisis.

Nvidia shares tumbled 14% on Friday, sliding for a seventh straight day as investors grew increasingly concerned about life after co-founder Jensen Huang. The stock closed lower for a seventh consecutive week, the longest such stretch since 2008.

Shares of Nvidia are down 46% over the period, pushing the company's market cap down by about $2.6 trillion. The stock is now trading at roughly 8.4 times forward earnings, while Advanced Micro Devices is valued at 29 times profit for the next 12 months.

On Friday, Reuters reported that the Securities and Exchange Commission is exploring potential accounting issues at Nvidia, pushing the stock down further.

Once the most valuable company in the world, Nvidia now has a market cap of $3 trillion, putting it fourth behind Apple, Microsoft and Alphabet.

Nvidia is reeling from the removal of Huang, who resigned on Nov. 12, after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who was allegedly a spy.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said earlier this week that Huang had resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

But the chipmaker's losing streak started before Nvidia was thrown into crisis as investors worried over the sustainability of the AI buildout, particularly after the midterm elections, and emerging competition in AI chips from Google and AMD.

Even a better-than-expected earnings report, which included an increase to fiscal 2028 guidance, didn't stem the selling. Interim CEO Colette Kress, the company's longtime CFO, projected revenue growth next fiscal year of 75%, and said that customers aren't going anywhere.

On Tuesday's earnings call with analysts, Kress signed off by saying, "we've got this." In a company meeting the next day, executives told staff that it was moving its yearly review process, which can come with more stock, from March to December.

For investors, one major question is whether Nvidia can continue to innovate and sustain its commanding lead in the AI chip market without its visionary leader. Almost the entire company now is built around the AI trade, a phenomenon that began taking hold just four years ago with the launch of OpenAI's ChatGPT.

Before that, Nvidia's primary business was selling graphics cards used for video games. In mid-2008, the last time the stock was this deep into a weekly losing streak, Nvidia was generating less than $1 billion per quarter. Now it pulls in more than $1 billion per day. At the time, the entire market was in freefall due to the worst financial crisis since the Great Depression.

#2849. Federal Reserve announces College Fed Challenge winners

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2846. Trump says he still has confidence in Lutnick

#2844. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#2842. 32 things we learned in NFL Week 11: 49ers leave no doubt as league's elite teams emerge

The 32 things we learned from Week 11 of the 2026 NFL season:

1. While Sunday night's matchup between the San Francisco 49ers and Minnesota Vikings pitted the league's No. 1 offense against its stingiest (in terms of points allowed) and, arguably, most ferocious defense, the Niners left no doubt who's currently atop the NFL mountain with their resounding, blitz-beating, 35-10 victory in Mexico City. A lot can happen between now and Valentine's Day, but the side effects of the Santa Clara substation might be the only thing standing in San Fran's path to a third Super Bowl appearance under brilliant HC Kyle Shanahan.

1a. Minnesota hadn't allowed more than 23 points in a game this season ... domestically anyway. But this is the third time the Vikings haven't scored more than 10.

1b. Fernando Mendoza, the No. 1 pick of the 2026 NFL Draft, is going to be experiencing a lot more firsts in the coming weeks now that he's elevated to QB1 of the Las Vegas Raiders. Sunday brought his first start against an AFC West rival, first start on the road, first regular-season TD pass – first three actually – and first INT, a fatal pick-six against the Denver Broncos. But make no mistake, Mendoza continues to show flashes, kept his team in a game against an opponent likely to be in the AFC's Super Bowl mix and – most importantly – survived an early baptism by fire against the Seattle Seahawks and Broncos, who feature two of the league's most formidable defenses.

2. The number of TD passes reeled in Sunday by Kansas City Chiefs TE Travis Kelce, nearly four years to the day since he last scored multiple times in a regular-season game. K.C. boat raced the Arizona Cardinals 34-7 on Mr. Swift's big day.

3. Number of sacks registered by DT Jeffery Simmons in the Chicago Bears' 21-13 defeat of the New Orleans Saints on Sunday. That's five sacks in three games since Simmons came to Chicago in what could still prove to be the biggest deal of the season, Kirk Cousins included.

4. The number of TD catches San Francisco All-Pro TE George Kittle has in two Mexico City appearances in his career, including two in Sunday night's bashing of the Vikings.

4a. Also the number of starting quarterbacks the Cardinals and Baltimore Ravens have used this season.

5. Jacoby Brissett is the common denominator between those teams, dealt from Arizona to Baltimore before this year's trade deadline. However, he lost his second start in six days with the Ravens on Sunday … despite giving them every opportunity to win during a wild game in Charlotte, which the Carolina Panthers won 35-33.

6. The number of NFL teams that were off this week, aka "bye-mageddon" in fantasy circles – the Atlanta Falcons, Cleveland Browns, Green Bay Packers, Los Angeles Rams, New England Patriots and Seahawks all idle in the league's lightest scheduling week of the season.

7. The number of touchdowns the Dallas Cowboys scored in their 51-30 rout of the Tennessee Titans.

8. The number of possessions Dallas had Sunday, one kneel-down by QB Dak Prescott notwithstanding, Brandon Aubrey booting a field goal on the one legit drive when the Cowboys didn't find the end zone.

9. The number of international games the NFL staged this season, an all-time record, the final game abroad in 2026 played Sunday night in Mexico City's Estadio Banorte between the Vikings and 49ers.

9a. The Niners improved to 5-1 in international games − 2-0 this season − and their .833 winning percentage tops among teams with at least five appearances abroad.

10. The number of international games the NFL could play annually going forward, at least under the terms of the current collective bargaining agreement, which runs through the 2030 season. Said another way, expect 10 international games in 2027.

11. Back to the Cowboys, who won by three TDs … but still surrendered 30 points and 346 yards with DC Christian Parker not calling the plays. Both figures represent the second-best outputs of the season for the Titans, who entered Sunday with the league's second-worst offense. Also, add two-hand touch issues to Dallas' endemic tackling issues.

12. The number of consecutive regular-season games the Pittsburgh Steelers have lost in Philadelphia. They last beat their intra-state rival Eagles on the road in 1965, roughly 15 months prior to the first Super Bowl.

13. But you're bound to lose any time 42-year-old QB Aaron Rodgers is your leading rusher … with 29 yards.

14. The number of times Cardinals TE Trey McBride was targeted in Sunday's defeat at Kansas City. (He caught nine of them but only for 54 yards.)

15. The number of NFL teams new Arizona QB1 Josh Johnson has been a member of – he didn't play for all of them – but any veteran as wise as this 40-year-old knows feeding McBride is the best course of action when you join a new team with an All-Pro pass catcher.

15a. The number of TD passes Johnson has thrown in his 14-year career (though it's worth noting he was actually drafted in 2008, fifth round by the Tampa Bay Buccaneers).

16. Who says 347-pound Bucs NT Vita Vea doesn't have abs?

17. The jersey number, at least most recently, for QB Daniel Jones, fired by the Indianapolis Colts after they were smoked 26-3 by the Houston Texans on Thursday night. And while Jones' next NFL employer is already a subject of debate, is a guy only a year removed from an Achilles tear and fresh off football shellshock in Indy really the answer for anyone in 2026?

18. The number of starts Cousins could potentially make this season given his trade from Vegas to Minnesota may confer him an extra game rather than a bye.

19. The number of points Philadelphia scored in their 19-16 defeat of the Steelers, the second most the Eagles have posted since QB Jalen Hurts was lost for the season after Week 5.

20. Congrats to Philly for breaking a seven-game slide and also to third-year QB Joe Milton, who notched his first regular-season victory.

21. As for the Steelers? At least their loss to the Eagles wasn't a direct result of WR Makai Lemon (1 catch for 3 yards), whom Philadelphia EVP/GM Howie Roseman scooped from Pittsburgh with the 20th overall selection of this year's draft – one spot before Steelers GM Omar Khan planned to take him.

22. However, Pittsburgh might need another wideout for its Black Friday game against the Broncos if coach Mike McCarthy opts to discipline DK Metcalf, who was ejected in the third quarter of the Steelers' defeat at Lincoln Financial Field. Didn't matter much from a performance standpoint given Metcalf had one reception for 2 yards on Sunday.

23. The New York Jets (3-8) suffered a second straight devastating win at SoFi Stadium … in terms of their pending draft position. They beat the Rams there in 2020, despite entering the game with an 0-13 record – the victory effectively knocking them out of the Trevor Lawrence sweepstakes atop the 2021 draft. Sunday, they edged the Chargers 27-24 – meaning the Bolts (1-9) stand a much better shot at obtaining Ohio State WR Jeremiah Smith at or near the top of the board … and/or holding the Jets hostage for draft capital given Gang Green's obvious need for a quarterback next spring.

24. The age of Detroit Lions RB Jahmyr Gibbs, who scored two TDs on the ground in Sunday's 31-21 defeat of Tampa Bay. Gibbs now has 52 career rushing TDs, tying him with Lions legend and Hall of Famer Barry Sanders for the most in league history by a player yet to reach his 25th birthday.

25. The Panthers are the only current division leader that has been outscored on the season.

26. Yet along with the Niners, Carolina is also the only team in the NFC with a two-game lead in the divisional win column.

27. The Buffalo Bills, Broncos and Lions have all win six in a row, and the Cincinnati Bengals' heater could match that if they knock off the Washington Commanders on Monday night.

28. The Bears have won five in a row and have not trailed throughout that hot streak, which coincides with QB Caleb Williams' Week 6 return from his hamstring injury.

29. But as the playoff picture in both conferences begins to crystallize, much of the intrigue seems to be in the middle of the pack. In the AFC, for instance – and all due respect to the Ravens and Patriots, both without their QB1s – the Steelers, Browns and Texans will quite likely wind up fighting for the final two wild-card spots.

30. The NFC should provide more high-quality drama. While the NFC East and South will almost surely be one-bid divisions, the NFC North and West ought to create plenty of tension as their teams vie for the final five playoff spots – some quality clubs sure to be left behind. Wednesday night's matchup, the first the NFL has ever played on Thanksgiving Eve, will pit the Packers and Rams in a contest that could have serious ramifications come January.

31. As we approach Thanksgiving, many – some? – will also flash back to that Tryptophan-induced nightmare that was the Butt Fumble … which celebrated its 14th anniversary Sunday. Maybe the Jets beating the Chargers isn't so bad after all.

32. RIP, Tom Jackson – a great player, analyst and person, the outpouring of love, respect and grief from ESPN telling you everything you need to know.

All the NFL news on and off the field. Sign up for USA TODAY's 4th and Monday newsletter.

#2839. How China is using summits to expand its global influence

SHENZHEN, China — For a second year in a row, Chinese President Xi Jinping has brought Russian President Vladimir Putin together with another major world leader on mainland Chinese shores.

U.S. President Donald Trump had a trilateral meeting with Xi and Putin on Thursday, after the three met the prior evening during a welcome banquet for the Asia-Pacific Economic Leaders' Meeting in Shenzhen. It marked the first time they had met together in person.

In September 2025, Xi, Putin and Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi briefly laughed together for a friendly photo-op alongside an annual meeting of the Shanghai Cooperation Organization in Tianjin, China. The gathering followed easing tensions between Beijing and New Delhi.

China's "ability to engage directly with the world major powers" reflects the country's influence across the Global South and Eurasia, said Jodie Wen, postdoctoral fellow at the Center for International Security and Strategy at Tsinghua University. "China hopes to play a more constructive role in this conflict-[ridden], unstable world."

In the future, "China will hope to provide more platforms" within its borders for countries to discuss issues, she said.

Trump, Xi, Putin said they plan to boost oil production, revamp UN after trilateral talks

I watched Trump, Xi and Putin gather in China. Here are my top takeaways

Trump holds talks separately with Putin and Xi ahead of their historic trilateral meeting

Takaichi gets chilly reception in China on first Japan leader visit since 2019

Why the Middle East is watching the Trump-Xi-Putin summit

Trump arrives in China for historic meeting with Xi, Putin

China's Xi calls for removing trade and investment barriers

Putin says EU is losing industry, falling behind competition

Three things to watch as Trump, Xi and Putin meet in China

China has increasingly played the role of broker, for instance, providing the forum for restoring diplomatic ties between Saudi Arabia and Iran in 2023.

By participating in and hosting different multilateral organizations, China has stayed in the Western orbit while cultivating relations with non-Western countries. The two photo-ops last year and this year reflect only a portion of Xi's large number of meetings with different world leaders.

Xi has hosted more than 40 heads of state so far this year, including at least eight around the APEC meetings in Shenzhen this week. That's on top of receiving over 60 state visits in 2025, according to CNBC analysis of official Chinese readouts.

Domestic Chinese media have highlighted how Xi has met with many foreign leaders around the China-hosted events.

"Beijing sees these multilateral platforms as opportunities to expand its international influence and demonstrate its commitment to multilateralism, while bilateral diplomacy remains equally important," said Yue Su, principal economist for China at EIU.

But she noted that different countries increasingly pursue different geopolitical and economic priorities, making it challenging to reach consensus even through multilateral organizations.

"Against this backdrop, China's diplomatic priorities are increasingly focused on delivering tangible economic benefits through trade, investment, financial connectivity and technology cooperation," she said.

On Thursday, Xi announced at the APEC Economic Leaders' Meeting that China would provide $20 million in support for the Asia Pacific region. He also advocated for the development of an "Asia-Pacific community with a shared future," an iteration of his commonly used slogan on a "shared future" for humanity more broadly.

The Shenzhen summit can create meaningful outcomes for crisis management, as well as diplomatic space for future negotiations, Tsinghua University's Wen said. She added that it's important for leaders to come together in person in order to avoid misunderstandings and further tensions.

Trump said Thursday at the trilateral that the U.S., China and Russia are "uniquely positioned" to discuss reducing nuclear weapons at their historic meeting. A Chinese readout subsequently said the three leaders discussed Ukraine, the Middle East and the Korean Peninsula.

—CNBC's Matthew Tan contributed to this report.

#2838. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2836. Payton Pritchard has to work through his struggles

Payton Pritchard has not yet met his own high standards this year.

His numbers are down across the board. He's dropped from 17 PPG on 46/38/89 splits last season to 13.4 PPG on 41/34/86, having one of the least efficient seasons of his career so far. Saturday's loss to the Kings was another rough night, and it came when Boston needed him most.

Pritchard went 4-for-17 from the field and 0-for-8 from three in his first start of the season. It's his fifth time shooting below 30% in 17 games.

With Jayson Tatum and Derrick White out, the expectation is that Pritchard takes on more of the scoring load. Struggling against Sacramento, which ranks 27th in defensive rating, is unusual. This is a matchup where you'd normally expect a big night. Instead, Boston got dragged into a dogfight that came down to the ball in his hands.

His final two shots showed where things went wrong. He hit a go-ahead jumper with 11 seconds left, then airballed at the buzzer with the game on the line.

With the floor cleared for him, he settled for a heavily contested step-back a foot inside the three-point line instead of attacking the rim. Pritchard didn't defend the shot afterward.

"Didn't get to a good enough spot," Pritchard said. "Probably need to try to find a better look there."

That was the bigger issue all night. Sacramento allows the highest field goal percentage at the rim in the league, a staggering 76.6%. Boston needed rim pressure, and Pritchard took just two shots there.

Even his 8-to-0 assist-to-turnover ratio is a bit misleading. One assist came when he tossed Neemias Queta a grenade late in the clock after failing to create space. There were several other occasions where he missed the roller, making his lack of turnovers more about being overly cautious.

He finished -13, the only Celtics starter in the negative. Plus-minus can be noisy in a single game, but this one matched the eye test. Meanwhile, his new mentor Mike Conley picked up the slack with a season-high 19 points and nearly saved the game.

It hasn't been all bad for Pritchard. But outside of two big games against the Bulls with 30 and 28 points, he hasn't been as advertised. Coming off a career year and a new contract extension, the expectation for him was another leap. So far it's looked closer to regression.

Pritchard didn't hide his own disappointment.

"As many good games as I'ma have, I have bad games, too," Pritchard said. "And tonight was a bad game. So, I look forward to bouncing back."

He usually finds his footing, and a couple of good games in a row can get him there. The Kings looked like a good launch pad. That wasn't the case, but there's a quick turnaround tonight against the Warriors, and a chance to wipe the slate clean

"It can either make you or break you," Pritchard said. "So go back to work and figure it out."

#2834. Cam Skattebo renews backflip celebration days after vowing to shelve it: 'It's hard to hold back from'

New York Giants running back Cam Skattebo vowed in the aftermath of Aidan Hutchinson's season-ending celebration injury that he was done doing backflips. That promise lasted less than four quarters.

Skattebo iced New York's 23-18 win over the Jacksonville Jaguars on Sunday with a run for a first down at the 2-minute warning that allowed the Giants to finish the game in victory formation.

When the play was done, Skattebo stood up and flexed. Then he did a backflip.

That was four days after Skattebo said this regarding backflips in the aftermath of learning that Hutchinson tore his ACL while jumping in celebration of a sack:

"It's hard to see," Skattebo said Wednesday of Hutchinon's injury, per the New York Post. "I'm saying yeah. Mentally, I'm like, 'I'm not doing that.' That ain't going to happen to me."

Per the Post, Skattebo reserved the right to renew his signature celebration "if we go to the playoffs or something."

Sunday's game must have fallen under "or something."

Skattebo doesn't have to look to Hutchinson as an injury reference point. He sustained his own season-ending injury as a rookie, a dislocated ankle that left him in fear that he'd need to have his foot amputated.

He, of course, did not lose his foot and was back to backflipping over the summer before returning to football — or he was trying, at least.

The backflip then made multiple on-field appearances prior to his vow to stop them. He was asked about the decision to renew the flip at his locker postgame.

"When energy calls for it, it calls for it," Skattebo said. "My teammates, I do whatever I can to bring the energy for the team. I play this game with my heart and soul.

"When that happens, it's second nature and it's hard to hold back from."

Giants head coach John Harbaugh was also asked about Skattebo's backflip at his postgame news conference.

"He couldn't help himself, could he. "He' just couldn't help himself."

#2831. The 'debasement trade' returns after Bessent bond maneuver. Crypto and gold are back in style

The debasement trade is gaining new traction on Wall Street as concern over the size and cost of the budget deficit mounts.

The conventional wisdom behind the trade is that perceived hard assets, like cryptocurrencies and precious metals, gain as investors try to hedge against a weaker U.S. dollar and Treasury debt in the face of ballooning government spending. Those fears reached a fever pitch last week before, and after, the Treasury Department's unusual step of increasing debt buybacks under Secretary Scott Bessent.

"The size of the Treasury purchases announced so far by Bessent are trivial in comparison to the size of the overall market, but the [signaling] effect was very powerful," said Stephen Coltman, head of macro at 21Shares, a crypto-focused creator of exchange-traded funds.

Gold touched three-month highs Monday, building on last week's advance of more than 5%. The yellow metal has climbed for five straight weeks and in August is on track for its biggest monthly rise since 1999.

Bitcoin added 2% Monday, to the highest since May. Last week, the digital currency soared 22%, its biggest three-day rally since 2023. Overnight Tuesday, the crypto touched $80,000.

The U.S. dollar index, which tracks the dollar against six other leading currencies, hit three-month lows last week and recorded its third down week in the last four. The index was little changed on Monday as investors expressed little appetite to bid up the greenback.

The Treasury Department said last week that it would double the maximum size of its bond buyback, to at least $4 billion from $2 billion. Two senior Treasury officials told CNBC Monday that the department could use its General Account to help fund the plans.

Last week's announcement followed news that the monthly U.S. budget deficit in July reached a five-year high and came at the same time as total federal government debt topped $40 trillion. Bessent told CNBC last week that he wields a "big toolkit" to calm the government bond market amid concerns about the government's financial health.

Long-dated U.S. Treasury yields surged last week, at one point sending the 30-year yield to almost a 20-year high of 5.34%, up from 4.82% in late June. Yields dipped and then rebounded in the wake of the Treasury buyback move, a signal that bond investors believed Bessent's moves were inadequate.

"Markets are saying something," the billionaire philanthropist and former energy trader John Arnold said in a Friday post on X. The weaker dollar, lower Treasury prices and strengthening hard assets, are "all part of the debasement trade," he wrote.

The Treasury's moves could aid the bond market, but also translate into outsized pain for the dollar, according to Nohshad Shah, Citadel Securities' head of fixed income sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

The stakes are high for monetary policymakers, as a weaker greenback can ease financial conditions, Shah said. U.S. inflation — above the Federal Reserve's 2% target for five years — could worsen.

The Fed might need to hike interest rates in response, Shah said. Fed funds futures reflect about a 56% chance the central bank raises borrowing costs at its October meeting, up more than seven percentage points from a week ago, according to CME's FedWatch tool.

"The bond market's message is straightforward: fiscal or monetary policy should be tighter," Shah said in a Monday note. "Households may ultimately pay for policymakers' unwillingness to fix the roof whilst the sun is shining."

Coltman at 21Shares said the appetite for alternative stores of value also stems from mounting geopolitical tension. The U.S. on Monday rolled out a global sanctions program focused on segregating Iran from the global economy, days after slapping 50% tariffs on billions of dollars in Canadian exports.

Still, assets that would benefit from a debasement trade have recently received votes of confidence on Wall Street.

Deutsche Bank analyst Michael Hsueh said in a Monday note that gold could surpass his target price of $4,800 an ounce. Already, that level suggests the precious metal only need to rise another 3% or so from where it closed on Friday. "We see the Treasury policy change as underlining the gold constructive view," Hsueh said.

Billionaire investor Ray Dalio recommended investors remain overweight gold and bitcoin ahead of what he warned could become a debt crisis in the U.S. as a result of unchecked government spending. Gold could account for as much as 15% of a model portfolio, the Bridgewater Associates founder said.

"The government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote on LinkedIn Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Others are less sure, devaluing the notion of a debasement trade.

It's probably too early to endorse the idea unless it's clear that the Fed will go along with the Treasury Department, said Alexander Lis, investing chief at Social Discovery Ventures.

#2829. I never wanted the American Dream and I felt like something was wrong with me—until I moved abroad

I got my first job at 17, working the register at an Eckerd's drugstore in Texas. My manager was only a few years older than me, and while my coworkers seemed motivated by the idea of working their way up to his position, I felt no desire to follow that path.

It all felt suffocating: the added responsibility, the longer hours, the idea that this was the ladder I was supposed to want to climb. I wanted to work, but I couldn't wrap my head around building my whole identity around a job.

I never wanted the American Dream I saw so many people around me striving to achieve. For a long time, I wondered if something was wrong with me. It wasn't until I finally made a long-term move abroad about a decade ago that I realized it was okay to want something different.

After college, I got a job in travel sales. I was good at it. Talking about travel all day suited the wanderlust I'd carried since my first trip abroad in my teens.

I continued working in sales, marketing and the odd temp role for years. But they were always just jobs. I didn't feel like they defined me or feel a calling to an industry or title the way people around me seemed to.

Of course, I wanted financial stability, but I was unwilling to sacrifice my time and well-being to get it. I just didn't connect with the version of ambition that meant grinding toward a corner office. What I wanted the most wasn't status; it was freedom, growth and new experiences.

The wanderlust eventually caught up to me, and at 26 I moved to Spain to teach English. Even though I was working, people saw it as taking a year off from my career and warned me it could damage my resume. I simply didn't care. Living for my resume had never made sense to me.

Looking back, I think being abroad offered an escape from the pressure to chase the traditional path of the American Dream. There, I felt free to enjoy life and figure out who I was without worrying about what box I needed to tick next.

I wanted to stay in Spain, but I couldn't find a visa that would let me live there long-term, so I returned to the U.S. I became a serial job hopper for almost 10 years, quitting a job every year to spend summers visiting friends in the Netherlands, the U.K. and Switzerland or solo traveling in Spain on the money I'd saved up.

On the personal side, I wasn't in a rush to get married or have kids. In my 30s, single and childfree, I felt like I was failing at adulthood, even though deep down, I didn't actually want the things I was supposedly behind on. Settling down just because everyone else was doing it never felt right to me.

And I couldn't shake the dream of living in Europe. In my 20s, people mostly assumed my desire to travel was a phase. By my 30s, the conversations had shifted and I was getting pulled aside. Friends, family and colleagues all had some version of the same message: It was time to get serious and build a life in Texas.

But my gut said to try one more time. So I did.

In 2015, on my fourth attempt, I finally made it to Spain for good. It was scary to shake things up when everyone around me was settling down, but deep down I knew at 35, this was my chance to stop trying to conform and start building a life that felt right for me.

In my new home, the pace was slower. I quickly noticed that people actually took their vacations. Strangers at a dinner party didn't immediately ask me what I did for work. Adults in their 30s, 40s and beyond were out living full social lives. Single, childfree women my age didn't feel like an anomaly.

Living abroad didn't solve everything overnight. I still had to figure out how to support myself. But having the space to define myself professionally without constant judgment changed what felt possible.

In 2017, I launched She Hit Refresh, where I help women over 30 figure out how to move to Europe and build new lives for themselves. It's not something I could have created from anywhere else, with any other experience than my own.

In the process, I've met so many others who left corporate America behind to create something different for themselves. I've seen more women asking the same questions I spent years asking myself. Burnt out, disillusioned with hustle culture, realizing that checking all the boxes of the American Dream they grew up on didn't deliver the feeling they were promised.

We're all figuring it out together; each of us writing a version of a life that didn't fit a standard template. Moving abroad helped me understand that success, for me, was never about earning more or owning more. It was always about more ease, more time, more space, more life.

For years, I thought I was behind. I felt like something was wrong with me for wanting a different kind of life. Looking back, I just hadn't found my place yet.

Cepee Tabibian is the founder of She Hit Refresh, where she's helped over 24,000 women age 30+ plan their move abroad. Grab her free Move Abroad After 30 Guide to start planning your own move. The daughter of Colombian and Iranian immigrants, Cepee grew up in Houston, Texas, before becoming an immigrant herself in Spain. Follow her @shehitrefresh.

#2827. Bright Side Debates: Jalen Green or Collin Gillespie?

It has not been an average Phoenix Suns season, despite the near .500 record through the first few games. With wins against the Houston Rockets and Denver Nuggets, the team showed they can compete with the best of the Western Conference. With blowout losses to the Portland Trail Blazers and the San Antonio Spurs, the squad showed how they often lack the chemistry needed to improve upon their surprising 45-win season a year ago.

But no matter whether the Suns win or lose, one question has consistently hovered: who should start in the backcourt next to Devin Booker? Jalen Green whose done so or Collin Gillespie, the team's first reserve.

Amid Green's recent struggles that have seen his minutes dip and the continued rumors around Dejounte Murray potentially being traded for him, the conversation has heated up over the past week, with the Suns' second unit that is led by Gillespie playing some of its best basketball and receiving praise from other teams.

So as the conversation continues to take over Suns media, we wanted to lay out the reasons for and against both to start to help paint a better picture of the conversation. Tom takes the side of Green being the starter while Holden goes with Gillespie.

When I evaluate this Suns team, I do so by attempting to project their playoff potential. Last year, 45 wins and escaping the Play-In tournament, courtesy of Jalen Green, was a wonderful surprise and a reason for some much-needed optimism after the Mike Budenholzer regime bottomed out. This season, getting swept by the Thunder, Rockets, Lakers, or even the Spurs would be a massive red flag flying over this current iteration of the Phoenix Suns. Our last two playoff opponents physically dominated us on the perimeter, running Booker off his spots, and turning us over and over and over again. When the pressure ratchets up, we need premier athletes in order to match up with the best of the West.Jalen Green, despite some inconsistencies, is a three-level scorer that can be, and has been, a deciding factor in winning games in the postseason. He did it in game two of the Rockets/Warriors series. He did it against Golden State again the very next year, when the Suns toppled the Warriors to advance through the play-in tournament. He was everything that night, posting an overwhelming offensive and defensive performance in a must-win postseason game and what would be the last victory the Suns could muster in the 2025-26 season. Even while getting swept by the Thunder, Green acquitted himself well. He put up 17, 21, 26, and 23 while nabbing 4 steals in the final game and totaling at least 5 rebounds every game of the series.

Jalen Green can break perimeter pressure and stack paint touches against even the best defenses, in a way that Collin Gillespie simply can't. The kind of downhill pressure Jalen can provide is invaluable to a team searching for ways to free up Devin Booker when teams make it their game plan to take him away. While Jalen's finishing leaves a little to be desired, teams have to respect his ability to get to the rim and yak-it-down right in their faces. The same can't be said for Collin, who went for 5 points against Portland in the play-in and followed up with 2 points in the game against Golden State. He didn't fare much better against the throttling perimeter pressure of the Thunder. Outside of coming alive for 20 points in the series finale, Gillespie posted 8, 7, and 7 points through the first three games, going 4/14 from three.

I view Jalen Green's early season struggles to find his rhythm in the starting lineup similar to how I view Khaman's struggles to find his footing in the league. What you give up now in wins and losses, you gain back at the end of the year by giving two physically gifted players the space and time to figure it out.

This season is about how the Suns finish the year, not about how they look 20 games in. Getting back to .500 gives the Suns the leash they need to allow Jalen Green to climb back to that ceiling that saw him take a young Rockets team to a two seed. Moving Jalen Green to the bench, after you ask him to sacrifice his game to play a facilitation role, would be peak selling-low on the second-best offensive player on the Suns' roster. Jalen Green has only played 46 games for the Suns. The best is yet to come, but Green will never hit those heights if he's relegated to playing a Malik Monk role. A role that Collin Gillespie is much better suited to play.

It's a little early to be analyzing the roster's ability to compete in the West only 20 games into the season, but if we are going to do that, a few things we should factor in.

Considering the talent upgrade at power forward with Miles Bridges, the continuity the roster has from a season ago, and the natural development that comes with a roster dominated by players in their 20s, it would be a disappointment if Phoenix got swept in an uncompetitive manner, as they did a season ago. If we're talking about playoff success built around a Devin-Booker-led unit, we need to talk about when he's thrived the most in the playoffs: when he's had a more traditional point guard in the starting lineup.

Since Chris Paul's departure following the 2022-2023 season, Booker hasn't played alongside many true point guards in the starting lineup outside of Tyus Jones. Phoenix has been swept in their two playoff appearances and a lot of that falls on how the Suns' offense has been predicated around Booker's burden to lead the offense while also needing to get himself involved. With Jalen Green in the lineup, Booker continues to start games out of position and not put his main offensive focus on what he's best at: scoring.

While Gillespie by no means is the talent or plays the same style as the Point God, he does allow Booker to slide back into shooting guard duties while playing alongside a guard that doesn't need to score or have the ball in their hands to produce. After a poor start to the season, Gillespie is shooting 39% from three this month compared to Green's 32%. He can add more consistent spacing than Green can.

Lastly, if we're looking at Jalen Green's playoff success as a whole. Yes, he has more experience and more highlights than Gillespie does, but consistently he's played well below his regular season outings. INCLUDING his strong 38-point performance in his second-ever playoff game, Green averaged just 13 points (nearly eight less than his regular season average that year), shooting 37% from the field and 30% from three. Outside of his big game, he didn't have another contest where he shot better than 38% from the field in his six other playoff games. And in his first playoffs with the Suns, he didn't do much better.

As much as the Suns need to develop the players they feel can help improve their playoff performance, they need to be thinking more about the players who help Devin Booker be the postseason killer he was earlier in his career. Gillespie's play style combined with Booker's familiarity with pass-first guards can give Booker the opportunity to play as he did in the postseason earlier in his career. Green would be better suited as the reserve who can carry the offensive scoring when Booker is on the bench.

We saw Collin Gillespie as a starter last season, and after 58 games started, the consensus, from the legacy media to the message boards, was that he wore down. His three-point percentage, in a record-setting year, fell from 42% to 39% from January to February, from 39% to 36% from February to March, and bottomed out at 25% in the five games the Suns played in April.What we've seen early from Collin off the bench is an ascending shooter making his case for the 6MOTY award and building a potentially special tandem with Luke Kennard. Off the bench, Collin is that guy. He's the number one or number two option every time he touches the floor. In the starting lineup, he becomes the fourth wheel, locked into holding defenders on the three-point line while Booker gets to his spots in the mid-range. Collin is at his best off the leash, when he's aggressively seeking his shot, when he's pulling up early in the shot clock for a deep three simply because he had a feeling he was going to make it.The Suns have a really good bench. We've seen the Thunder ride a deep bench to a championship and a Western Conference Finals appearance. The Suns might have constructed a championship-caliber bench, but if they move the leader of the bench to the starting lineup in order to improve three-point shooting and find a ball-handler to free up Devin Booker, they could risk losing that advantage. Jalen Green is not Collin Gillespie. He's never come off the bench, whereas that's always been the trajectory of Collin's career. Jalen Green can try to adapt to a bench role, but Gillespie was born in it. The Suns will lose on both sides if they were to sit Jalen in favor of Collin.Jalen Green will struggle to find his rhythm in reduced minutes off the bench. Collin Gillespie will struggle to find his shot as he spends the majority of his minutes opening up looks for Devin Booker. That connection with Luke Kennard? Gone. The Suns are already having to scrape by with Oso starting, when he would be much more effective working against backup centers as the hub of the second unit. The Collin-Kennard-Oso trio could end up being the difference between a play-in appearance and a 5th seed, but not if we bail on Jalen before he's played 50 games for the team. Jalen Green is not a bench player, since he's not a facilitator. There is no path forward for Jalen off the bench except as a trade piece, and when we keep having to trade players because they don't work on the roster, we will continue to burn through what little assets we have left and reduce our ability to make a real impact addition to the roster.Collin Gillespie is a bench player, and he's a damn good one. Potentially one of the best in the NBA, not just this year, but for the next two to three years at least. Starting Collin diminishes his value and negatively impacts winning long term.

Tom is right. Collin Gillespie would be the Suns' fourth or fifth option in the starting lineup, but that's what the Suns need. Unlike last season, Phoenix has a starting power forward in Miles Bridges who is looking to create their own shot and does so consistently. Adding a fourth player who tends to score at an efficient clip takes away from the team's efficiency.

Out of the 248 different four-man lineups the Suns have played this year, when Brooks, Green, Booker, and Bridges all shared the floor, Phoenix is -74 in 221 minutes this season, their lowest plus-minus out of any four-man lineup the team has deployed this season by 25 points, according to Hoops Junkie. And while the Suns are -32 when they deploy the four-man lineup of Bridges, Gillespie, Booker, and Brooks, they've played just 32 minutes together, and Gillespie's floor spacing provides the three more room to operate on offense.

As we've seen throughout Jordan Ott's coaching tenure, he's not afraid to play reserves late, and even if Green is starting games on the bench, it doesn't mean he's going to end them sitting next to the coaches. Green played a few games off the bench last year when he was returning from his hamstring injury, but it would most definitely be an adjustment for him to start games with the second unit. However, as Green's continued to struggle to start the season, his minutes have gone down, and he's beginning to contribute in more ways than just as a scorer.

Just because Gillespie is starting doesn't mean he can't maintain his relationship with the second unit, and Green can have more room to operate as a scorer without impeding other shot creators' productivity. The identity of the bench and the team's ability to consistently play hard has shown it's able to endure lineup changes like they did earlier in the season when Khaman Maluach got replaced by Oso Ighodaro as the starting center.

The Suns are already carrying the weight of the Bradley Beal dead money, so it's difficult to convince me they can compete in the playoffs if they are bringing 36 million dollars off the bench.

Trade Jalen before you bench him, because $36 million in a matching contract is at least going to bring back a starter. There is no other team in the NBA that is spending that type of money on a bench player, and the teams that have a bench player making close to that much aren't very good. Jordan Poole is making $34 million for the Pelicans. Jerami Grant is making $34 million for an upstart Grizzlies team, but who really expects them to make the playoffs? Who thinks that's a good contract?

Like it or not, the Phoenix Suns have $36 million invested in Jalen Green, and anything other than a temporary stint on the bench handicaps the Suns' ability to field a starting lineup that can compete with the best in the Western Conference come playoff time. Jalen Green is a volume, rhythm shooter. He needs to get more shots, earlier in the game. He needs to be on the floor for longer periods of time in order to reach flow state. Despite what seems to be the consensus on the internet, Jalen Green is not going to get better coming in off the bench. Even if he did, it would be impossible for him to justify his contract playing twenty minutes and scoring 8-12 points a game. It's a losing proposition from the onset, no matter how well Jalen plays.

Instead of sabotaging the kid, as they did with this facilitation role, double down on him. Give him the green light you've given every other player on the team, from Brooks to Bridges to Collin to Goodie to Kennard. Why is Jalen Green the only player asked to do less of what he's great at? Why is he not extended the same opportunity to lean into the aspects of his game that make him special as the rest of the team?

The Suns need to at least attempt to get their money's worth from this contract, whether Green is on the team after the trade deadline or not. Anything less would be a disservice to the team, the fanbase, and Jalen Green.

I think NBA teams know who Jalen Green is. He's a dynamic, undersized, inefficient scorer that can get hot and stays confident no matter how he's shooting the ball. He's already been traded before and has spoken on numerous occasions about life in trade rumors. The Suns also didn't sign Green to his contract; the Rockets did. his salary was an essential part in sending Kevin Durant to the Houston Rockets. Moving Green to the second unit isn't an example of Phoenix devaluing a player that they signed.

If Green becomes a reserve, it won't impact his trade value. In contrast, it will give him more room to operate and allow him more opportunities to do what he does best: score. Jalen Green is asked to do less of what he's best at because what he does best he does at an inefficient clip and doesn't provide the defensive impact that other inefficient scorers do, like Dillon Brooks. If Green comes off the bench despite his large salary, it gives other organizations around the league the opportunity to see the Suns' willingness to make decisions purely based on basketball and not give in to other variables beyond what's best for the team's on-court success.

Potentially, if Green shows that he can thrive as a reserve, it can show not only other teams, but most importantly the Suns, his ability to adapt to new opportunities, independent of what his salary is. Albeit rare, as Tom pointed out, players like Jrue Holiday and Alex Caruso have found opportunities to thrive in the second unit despite their large salaries, and if Green can do so, it can be a positive reflection on how much the Suns' culture has improved the last two seasons, considering all the turmoil that Bradley Beal coming off the bench resulted in for the Suns in the 2024-25 campaign.

The Suns have built a strong culture that has been praised by people close and far based on sacrifice. Someone's contract length or size cannot dictate playing time. It would go against what the team has been trying to build since the departures of Kevin Durant and Bradley Beal, when the team switched up their culture and play style.

You've heard the key points for both to start; now you be the judge.

#2824. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#2819. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

Vinh ranks No. 123 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 28.50%. See Marvell Technology Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2817. I’ve studied over 200 kids—to avoid raising a people pleaser, ditch 6 parenting habits: It starts in ‘ordinary, well-intentioned moments’

We all want to raise children who are kind, respectful and considerate. But sometimes, the very behaviors we praise are the ones teaching our children to put themselves last.

A child who never complains, always agrees and goes out of their way to make others happy might seem like a parent's dream. But what if they've simply learned that being easy to love means trying to please everyone all the time?

As a conscious parenting coach who's worked with over 200 families, I've seen how people pleasing can begin in the most ordinary, well-intentioned moments at home.

Here are six parenting habits worth reconsidering.

We love telling our children how easygoing they are, especially when they rarely complain or make demands. But when being easy earns our approval, children may start believing their needs are a burden and their feelings are better kept to themselves.

What to do instead: Make room for your child's disappointment, frustration and disagreement, even when it's inconvenient. They need to know they don't have to be happy or agreeable to be accepted by you.

When children question our decisions, it's tempting to respond with "Don't talk back" or "Because I said so." But when we repeatedly shut down their opinions, we may be teaching them that having a voice comes at the cost of a connection with us.

What to do instead: Separate disrespectful behavior from differences in perspective. You can say, "You don't have to agree with me. I want to understand what you think."

Statements that start with "After everything I've done for you…" may seem harmless in the moment, but they can leave children feeling responsible for their parents' emotional well-being.

When children hear this sentiment after misbehaving, they may become more concerned with how to make us feel better instead of thinking about what they did wrong. And they can begin confusing being good with keeping other people happy.

What to do instead: Address your child's behavior without making them feel guilty for disappointing you. Children need to learn to take responsibility for their actions, not for managing their parents' emotions.

We praise children for sharing, compromising and letting others have their way. But when self-sacrifice is consistently celebrated, children may begin to believe that being a good person means putting themselves last.

What to do instead: Teach your child that kindness doesn't require self-sacrifice. Let them know it's okay to opt out, express what they want or choose not to share something. Someone else's disappointment doesn't automatically mean they've done something wrong.

We teach children to say "please," "thank you" and "sorry," but how often do we teach them that they're allowed to say "no"?

When children are expected to hug relatives, share everything or agree in order to avoid offending someone, they may learn that other people's comfort matters more than their own.

What to do instead: Let your child know they can disagree with an adult, decline a hug or express a different opinion without being impolite or disrespectful. Respect means treating others with consideration, not automatically doing what they want.

Children notice when we say yes even though we're overwhelmed, apologize for things that aren't our fault or avoid difficult conversations because we don't want to upset anyone.

We can tell our children to stand up for themselves all day long, but if they watch us constantly abandon our own needs to keep the peace, they'll learn something very different.

What to do instead: Let your children see you set boundaries, express your needs and respectfully disappoint people when necessary. Show them that saying no doesn't make someone selfish and that healthy relationships can withstand disagreement.

Raising a confident child means teaching them that being kind and caring for others doesn't have to come at the expense of being themselves. Because the goal isn't to raise a child who's liked by everyone. It's to raise one who doesn't have to lose themselves to be liked.

Reem Raouda is a certified conscious parenting coach, speaker and author dedicated to one core idea: that loving your child and making them feel safe are not the same thing. She is the founder of The Safe Mom and creator of The Safe Mom Masterclass, helping parents raise emotionally healthy children through emotional safety, connection and self-awareness. Find her on Instagram.
