Trevor Lawrence and the Jaguars suffered a stunning loss to the Giants on Sunday.

They did not. Cam Skattebo ran for a first down before the two-minute warning with the Jaguars having exhausted their final timeout. The Giants were able to kneel out the remaining two minutes of game clock in victory formation.

It added up to a jarring loss for the now 7-3 Jaguars on the heels of an aggressive trade deadline in which they made several big swings to bolster their hopes of a Super Bowl run. The Giants, meanwhile, improved to 6-3-1 to maintain their lead in the NFC East over the 5-6 Dallas Cowboys.

Coen was asked about his decision to kick the late field goal in his postgame news conference. He cited analytics charts and described going for a touchdown as a "hope play."

"We talked about it a few times," Coen said. "Our defense, they hadn't done anything up until that point. Everything about the charts and everything talked about it said kick the field goal, get within five points.

"Go get a stop, and you feel pretty good about where you're at. Just executing that down-and-distance on a fourth down is kinda challenging. Especially, they're zero blitzing you every play, the ball's gonna come out. It's like a hope play."

Coen's comments Sunday stood in contrast to a previous discussion with Barstool Sports about a strikingly similar, but not identical hypothetical scenario.

In that discussion, Coen was asked about whether to kick a field goal or go for it when down by eight points at the 8-yard line with one key caveat: "When you're up against the greatest quarterback of all time."

"You've gotta go for it," Coen said. … "It's weird down there — and you're in an opportunity to score a touchdown, you're that close. We all know how hard it is to score in the red zone in this league. … I think you've gotta be aggressive there."

Giants quarterback J.J. McCarthy is certainly not "the greatest quarterback of all time." It's not clear if Coen would have made a different decision if facing a different caliber of quarterback.

The Giants were in control virtually from the start. They took a 10-3 advantage into the second quarter and a 20-6 lead into halftime. While McCarthy kept New York's offense moving and scoring, the Jaguars repeatedly broke down in the red zone.

Jacksonville's first three trips to the red zone produced 6 points courtesy of two field goals and a turnover on downs in the third quarter when a fourth-and-1 pass from Trevor Lawrence fell incomplete.

Jacksonville's fourth red-zone trip produced controversy on a third-down effort to cut their deficit to one possession.

They appeared to do just that when Parker Washington made a leaping catch in the back of the end zone and got both feet in bounds. But officials ruled that he didn't maintain control of the ball as he hit the ground.

Coen used Jacksonville's last remaining challenge to challenge the call on the field. The ball moved in Washington's hands after Washington hit the ground, but he never appeared to lose control of it.

But officials didn't see enough to overturn the call on the field, and the ruling of an incomplete pass stood. The result was a fourth down in which the Jaguars settled for a field goal to cut their deficit to 20-9.

Jacksonville finally cracked the end zone with a touchdown pass from Lawrence to Brian Thomas Jr. with 6:39 remaining. A failed 2-point conversion left the Jaguars facing a 23-15 hole.

Then came the decision that will prompt plenty of questions for Coen.

In the end, the Jaguars woke up on offense and limited the Giants on defense as they outgained New York, 354 yards to 238. Lawrence completed 25 of 47 passes for 301 yards with 1 touchdown and 2 interceptions.

McCarthy completed 18 of 38 passes for 173 yards with 1 touchdown. But he didn't turn the ball over as the Giants won the takeaway battle, 2 to 1.

The result will leave the Jaguars with plenty to ponder ahead of a Week 12 home game against the Titans.

The Giants do indeed have the ability to run the clock out.

The discussion for this game will be Jacksonville focused. First it will be whether or not Parker Washington deserved a touchdown on his reception in the end zone that was ruled incomplete.

The second will be Liam Coen's decision to be on the 8-yard line facing fourth down and opting to kick a meaningless field goal rather than trying to tie the game.

Giants running back Cam Skattebo said that he was retiring the backflip after what happened to Aidan Hutchinson tearing his ACL celebrating a sack.

Skattebo picked up the first down at the two-minute warning. Jacksonville has no timeouts left, so this should be the ball game.

The Giants running back celebrated the only way he knew how: with a backflip.

The Giants are in the driver's seat needing a few first downs to seal with win against the Jaguars.

On second and seven, JJ McCarthy hit rookie wideout Malachi Fields for the 19-yard gain. Jacksonville only has one timeout left.

The Jaguars were grooving down the field, but the offense stalled in the red zone. After several incompletions by Trevor Lawrence to the end zone, Jacksonville opted to kick the field goal.

Cam Little connected on the 26-yard field goal. Jacksonville now trails 23-18 with 3:40 left.

After an incompletion on first down, JJ McCarthy hit Malik Nabers for an eight-yard gain. An offensive lineman was then whistled for unnecessary roughness for hitting a Jaguars defender late.

After another incompletion, New York was forced to punt.

Jacksonville gets the ball back down 8 with 5:47 left to go in regulation.

It looked like it was going to be a broken play as Trevor Lawrence had time, but couldn't find anybody.

All of a sudden he fired it to the end zone and Brian Thomas Jr. had gotten wide open.

Jacksonville decided to go for two. He floated it into the end zone but it was picked, which doesn't mean much for the stat sheet other than the attempt failed.

New York leads 23-15 with 6:39 left to go.

Giants rookie kicker Dominic Zvada had made a 53-yard field goal and missed a 32-yarder.

Zvada lined up for a 43-yard attempt and drilled it.

New York leads 23-9 with 10 minutes to go.

He had an incompletion on first down, and then threw his second interception of the game.

The officials in this game are certainly apparent.

New York picked up seven yards on first down, but it was called back due to offensive holding.

JJ McCarthy then had three consecutive incompletions. New York took 1:02 off the clock and punted back to Jacksonville.

The Giants lead 20-9 with 11:15 left in the game.

It looked like Parker Washington made his latest video game-esque catch, but it was ruled an incompletion.

Replay on the broadcast had JJ Watt swearing that it was a touchdown. Even the rules analyst on the broadcast said it should have been a touchdown. Jacksonville challenged the play and the ruling was upheld.

While the Jaguars didn't get a touchdown out of it, Jacksonville did add three with a field goal.

New York leads 20-9 with 12:17 left in the game.

New York's drive consisted of two incompletions and then JJ McCarthy scrambling for a massive 29-yard gain.

On third and 16 McCarthy had his third incompletion of the drive and New York was forced to punt.

Jacksonville takes over trailing 20-6 at the start of the fourth quarter.

Jacksonville got down to the 11-yard line. Trailing 20-6 with a minute to go in the third quarter, Liam Coen opted to go for it.

Trevor Lawrence stared down his target and tried to thread the needle to Jakobi Meyers, but the Giants defense was all over it for the turnover on downs.

The Jaguars defense had been on the field for over five minutes. Jacksonville needing something and it came in the form of a Cam Skattebo fumble.

Jacksonville scooped it, and the defender spun away from the line and took it back 42 yards to give the Jaguars great field position.

Jacksonville couldn't capitalize on New York's missed field goal. Trevor Lawrence had two incompletions sandwiching a one-yard gain by Bhayshul Tuten.

New York leads 20-6 with 9 minutes to go in the third quarter.

Rookie kicker Dominic Zvada had made a 53-yard field goal already in the game. It looked like his 32-yard attempt was going to be easy, but he planted weird and ended up pushing it wide left.

Giants head coach John Harbaugh is showing he trusts his rookie kicker.

Dominic Zvada has been inconsistent this season, but nailed a 54-yarder toward the end of the first half to put the Giants up 20-6.

Trevor Lawrence put on a magic show in the backfield. He eluded three different sets of pressure from the Giants and managed to get a pass off.

Brenton Strange caught it even though it wasn't nearly enough for the first down, it was still impressive. It ended up being just a gain of four.

After the two-minute warning, Jacksonville punted trailing 17-6.

This might be Abdul Carter's breakout game. He has found a mismatch on the Jaguars' offensive line because he is consistently going after Trevor Lawrence in the backfield.

Carter already has two sacks in the game and four tackles overall.

New York drove the field against the Jacksonville defense.

The Giants' nine-play, 66-yard touchdown drive ends with JJ McCarthy finding Malik Nabers in the back of the end zone as the wide receiver just manages to get his two touches in.

That was an unbelievable collapse by Detroit, and quite possibly the worst game of Cade Cunningham's career.

The Pistons went into the fourth quarter with an 85-74 lead. They outscored the Hawks by 10 in the third quarter, and ESPN had Detroit at a 90% chance to win the game when the final frame started.

That went downhill quickly as the Hawks went on an 18-0 run to open the fourth quarter. The Pistons didn't score their first points until Isaiah Joe hit a corner three with 4:52 left in the game. Detroit's offense stalled, the ball wasn't moving, and for whatever reason, Cade Cunningham and Daniss Jenkins took it upon themselves to try to iso Dyson Daniels and Lu Dort. In what should've been a victory tonight in Atlanta, Detroit blew their double-digit lead and deserved to lose this game. That was one of the worst fourth quarter performances I've ever watched, and it looked like a middle school team that couldn't grab a rebound in a scrimmage against the high school varsity.

Detroit moves to 10-6 on the year and will now travel to Orlando to take on the Magic on Tuesday night. Let's hope this Pistons team has a short memory and burns tonight's fourth quarter tape.

#2777. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

Barlow looks set for season debut with Sixers after long days, 'miserable' weeks originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Dominick Barlow is on the verge of his season debut with the Sixers.

The team listed Barlow as probable for its Saturday afternoon matchup with the Bucks at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Barlow practiced Friday and has been making clear progress in his recent on-court work recovering from an injury formally classified as a "lower back stress reaction."

He was a reliable rotation fixture last season, playing 71 regular-season games and starting 59. Barlow posted 7.7 points, 4.8 rebounds, 1.2 assists and 0.9 steals per contest in 23.8 minutes per game. The Sixers then made a common-sense call to exercise his $3.4 million option for the 2026-27 season.

The 23-year-old described his back injury as "an overuse thing."

"I guess there was an area or maybe I started my (offseason) workouts too early," he said. "I don't know what it was fully in terms of what caused it. Once it happened, it happened. Nothing I can do about it besides try to correct it and get my body feeling as good as possible."

He called the past few weeks "miserable" as he rehabbed and waited on the green light by the Sixers' training staff.

"The days just felt longer," Barlow said. "Trying to be engaged mentally, which I think I've done a good job with, but the physical aspect wasn't there. I'm somebody that's prided himself on competing, being available. Not being able to do that was hard, but I'm excited to kind of put this past me."

With LeBron James and Jaylen Brown on the team now, Barlow won't start at forward when the Sixers are at full strength.

He's still a multi-dimensional frontcourt piece for Sixers head coach Nick Nurse who may very well get substantial opportunities at backup center.

"Just cutting, playing hard, being ready to make the open shot," Barlow said. "And then I don't think you can ever have too much defensive versatility. I think that's something I can bring, try to help us be more versatile."

When Barlow checks into his first game of the season, he'll appreciate the moment.

"You don't really realize what you have until it's not there (anymore)," he said. "I'm just excited to play. …. Just playing the first stint … that's going to be the main thing I'm looking forward to."

A Sixers official said the following players were all "unrestricted" at the team's light practice:

Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness)

Bona still needs to practice with contact before a return is on the table, according to the official. Saturday's game will mark his eighth consecutive absence.

#2773. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2770. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#2769. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2768. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2765. 26-year-old Google worker earns $323,000 a year—but lives on less than minimum wage

This story is part of CNBC Make It's Millennial Money series, which details how people around the world earn, spend and save their money.

At 26 years old, Google AI security analyst and YouTuber Charles Broomfield is on track to earn about $323,000 this year — but he lives on less than minimum wage.

For the past few years, Broomfield has kept his annual spending on core living expenses under $20,000, less than the roughly $23,700 a full-time worker would earn at the minimum wage, averaged across all 50 states.

He does it by living with roommates in Washington, D.C., walking or biking instead of driving, eating free meals at work and rarely spending beyond his basic needs. Even when he splurges on something fun, he keeps things modest; in August, that meant a baseball game and a single $7 beer.

"I guess something else that probably does make me a little bit unique is I really don't feel joy when I buy things," he says. "I feel like I have what I need."

There is a goal behind all that saving. Broomfield has pursued financial independence since college and now expects to reach his target of $750,000 in investments within the next year, well ahead of his original goal of doing so by age 30. As his income has climbed, his lifestyle has largely stayed the same, he says.

Here's how Broomfield keeps his spending so low and what he plans to do once he no longer needs the paycheck.

Broomfield grew up in Colorado Springs, Colorado, the son of two military veterans. He and his siblings had to set aside half of everything they earned growing up. They could spend the other half or earn interest from their parents by putting that money away, too.

By his teenage years, Broomfield had become a disciplined saver. At the time, he dreamed of using his cash stash to buy some of the familiar trappings of wealth.

"I wanted a Lamborghini and I wanted some nice penthouse," he says. "I just wanted them because I thought other people wanted them."

At 17, Broomfield discovered Mr. Money Mustache, a personal finance blog by a former software engineer who retired at 30. The blog advocates living frugally and investing much of your income to build a portfolio large enough to support you through withdrawals, without needing a paycheck. It introduced Broomfield to the financial independence, retire early — or FIRE — movement and gave all that saving a new purpose.

"All I had to do was live super frugally and I could actually reach financial freedom by the time I was 30 and go do whatever actually made me happy in life," he says.

The idea stuck with him. After graduating from Knox College in 2022, Broomfield moved to Northern Virginia for a job paying more than $100,000 a year and set a goal of reaching $750,000 in investments before 30 while spending less than $25,000 a year.

He also decided to start at $0. Broomfield had roughly $50,000 in savings when he graduated, with his parents having covered the portion of his college tuition not paid for by scholarships. He wanted his FIRE total to reflect only what he earned after college.

In his first year after college, Broomfield was "super geeked out" about reaching financial freedom, he says.

"It's almost the only thing I thought about, because I was building all these new habits and making real money for really the first time in my life," he says.

That enthusiasm sometimes took him to extremes. He even considered living in a van parked in his employer's garage to avoid paying rent.

"I crunched the numbers on it, and effectively every year that I spent living in a van rather than paying rent would allow me to retire approximately one month faster," he says. "It just [didn't] seem really worth it to go to that extreme."

But Broomfield still found satisfaction in squeezing every dollar out of his budget. He once biked two miles to a grocery store to save $1 on toothpaste rather than buy it at the Whole Foods next to his apartment.

"For a lot of people, they feel their wins by buying stuff for themselves," Broomfield says. "I get my wins by seeing how much I can invest."

Looking back, Broomfield acknowledges that some of his early efforts to save money made little difference in how quickly he could reach financial independence.

"I never expect anybody else to do exactly what I'm doing," he says. "I know I'm way more of a geek about this than most people, and I'm particularly well positioned to be able to live this kind of life."

Broomfield's core living expenses totaled $1,477 in August, excluding savings, investments and payments toward his graduate school tuition.

Here's a breakdown of where his money went that month, including those additional payments:

Roughly 90% of Broomfield's income went to savings and investments in August, a fairly typical month for him, he says. He put about $7,931 into his 401(k) and another $10,000 into a taxable brokerage account.

Another $700 went toward a 0% interest employer tuition repayment plan with a former employer, leaving him with a remaining balance of $16,800 in August.

Housing is Broomfield's biggest expense, though he keeps costs down by sharing an apartment with four roommates. In a neighborhood where one-bedroom apartments commonly rent for more than $2,000, the arrangement saves him about $1,000 a month.

One thing Broomfield is willing to splurge on is a martial arts gym membership. He pays $229 a month to train as a kickboxer, even though he could work out for free at Google's office gym. Broomfield considers it a "young person's sport" and doesn't want to postpone competing until after he reaches financial independence. It's also a way to socialize with other amateur fighters who share his interest.

Broomfield doesn't own a car and generally walks, bikes or takes public transportation. Google covers his daily Metro commute, which helps keep his transportation costs low. He tries to meet friends near Metro stations and, when visiting his grandfather in Virginia, takes public transit as far as he can before using Uber for the last leg of the trip.

Before joining Google, Broomfield typically spent around $200 a month on food. Now that he eats most of his meals at work, he spends about $70 a month on average. His $28 food bill in August was especially low because he had ingredients left over from July.

He also cooks less at home these days. Previously, he relied more on batch cooking, including pressure cooker chili that makes nine servings for about $2 each. For snacks, he might have a bowl of Raisin Bran or celery with peanut butter.

“I did the math once, and peanut butter was the lowest cost thing per gram of protein in the entire grocery store,” he says in a video on his YouTube channel, which he started in 2023 to document his FIRE journey.

For entertainment, he relies on the free version of YouTube, where he watches educational content about startups, business and entrepreneurship, or listens to music.

Broomfield expects to make about $323,000 in 2026, more than triple the roughly $100,000 he earned in his first year out of college. His compensation rose to about $120,000 over his first three years, then jumped to $200,000 when he joined Amazon Web Services in 2025. After a brief stint at Capital One, he moved to Google this year. By keeping his expenses low, investing most of what he earns and riding an upward-trending stock market, he has built up about $650,000 in investments since graduating from college.

Broomfield earns money from his YouTube channel as well, which has expanded to offer personal finance and career advice. About $48,000 of his projected income this year will come from the channel and a related career-coaching business.

Even as his income has grown, Broomfield has stuck with roommates, public transportation and the same modest spending habits.

"I've really separated how happy I am from how much money I spend," he says. "I truly don't believe that I would be significantly happier if I spent a lot more money."

Much of the money he doesn't spend goes straight into investments. He maxes out tax-advantaged accounts including his 401(k) and HSA, and has sometimes contributed 100% of his paycheck to his 401(k) early in the year to reach the annual limit as quickly as possible.

Broomfield keeps his investment strategy simple, favoring broad-market index funds.

"I don't try and pick individual stocks. I just don't have the time to do the research and understand the risk and potential returns of those individual stocks," he says. "And the research shows that most people who try to pick individual stocks don't end up doing better than if they just invested in the stock market as a whole."

He avoids cryptocurrency for a similar reason. Broomfield says he doesn't understand what gives it value and doesn't want to put money into investments he doesn't understand.

At his current pace, Broomfield expects to reach his $750,000 investment goal within the next year.

When Broomfield discovered the FIRE movement at 17, he worried about being stuck in what he imagined as a "soul-crushing" 9-to-5 office job until age 65. He saw financial independence as a way to quit working altogether.

But while financial independence would give him enough money to live without a paycheck, Broomfield no longer sees early retirement as the goal.

“Once I reach financial freedom, maybe this is a little ironic, but I actually think I will keep working,” he says.

His YouTube business already earns enough in most months to cover his expenses without a full-time job. With that income and his growing investments, Broomfield hopes to eventually build a business of his own, potentially in AI security.

"Having complete ownership and complete buy-in to something that's my own. That sounds really exciting to me," he says.

Whatever he chooses to do, Broomfield sees financial independence as a way to keep his options open. He bases his savings goal on the so-called “4% rule, a retirement guideline that suggests withdrawing 4% of your investments in the first year, then adjusting withdrawals for inflation.

For Broomfield, that would mean roughly $30,000 a year from a $750,000 portfolio. That's enough to cover his current expenses, though he recognizes that could change if he eventually starts a family.

"There's a very good chance I won't want to live the way I do forever. That's one of the reasons I'm doing it now. I'm young. I'm healthy. I'm single," he says. "I totally understand that somebody with a family probably doesn't want to live with a bunch of roommates."

But the money Broomfield has already saved gives him more freedom to choose where and how much he works without feeling tied to a paycheck.

"Having that peace of mind to know that I'll be fine, even if I can't find work quickly or even if I get laid off tomorrow, that has just been a huge breath of fresh air for myself," he says.

#2764. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2762. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.

#2761. Quantum computing’s first real-world payoff may come in energy, not drug discovery

For years, the case for quantum computing has rested on ambitious promises, from discovering new drugs by simulating how molecules behave to optimizing supply chains and airline schedules. Many of the biggest aims are still likely to be years, if not decades, away.

But quantum software, which uses algorithms designed to solve complex problems faster than conventional computers, is already being tested on a more immediate challenge: the energy transition, from keeping power grids stable to finding better materials for solar cells and hydrogen production.

Phasecraft, a quantum software company founded in Bristol, England, in 2019, is one of the firms putting the technology to work in energy. It raised $34 million in Series B funding in 2025, and in September this year announced plans to set up U.S. headquarters in Arlington, Virginia.

There are enormous opportunities to solve problems that are both important for energy security and commercially valuable, Ashley Montanaro, co-founder and CEO of Phasecraft, told CNBC.

Phasecraft isn't alone in applying quantum software to the energy industry.

Quantinuum, which was formed by the merger of Cambridge Quantum and Honeywell Quantum Solutions and listed on Nasdaq in June, is using the technology to model materials for hydrogen fuel cells for transportation. It is also working with partners on materials for carbon capture.

Another U.K. player, ORCA Computing, a spinout from the University of Oxford, has partnered with BP to better understand how a molecule's physical, chemical and biological properties are governed. That could help explain how biofuels burn and how to make them burn more efficiently

In a separate project, ORCA worked with Japanese software company JIJ on a challenge power companies face: deciding which generators to switch on or off, and when, to meet demand at the lowest cost.

That project also involved BP and the National Quantum Computing Centre (NQCC), the U.K.'s national laboratory for quantum computing, which is part of the government-backed U.K. Research and Innovation. The involvement of the NQCC, like that of ARPA-E, suggests governments are paying close attention to quantum software's potential role in energy, as are companies such as BP.

For Phasecraft, a priority is grid stability and optimization. It has partnered with the National Energy System Operator (NESO), the independent system planner that balances energy supply and demand in England, Scotland and Wales, on grid optimization tasks, including managing power exchanges and isolating network sections during emergencies. The company says its software can complete these tasks up to 1,000 times faster than conventional computing.

Phasecraft believes its grid software could be particularly sought after in Asia, where electrification is rapidly expanding, increasing the need for grid resilience to balance high and growing demand with the intermittent supply offered by renewables.

The company is also working with U.K. solar cell maker Oxford PV on the development of new materials for photovoltaic cells capable of operating more efficiently than existing ones.

Separately, it has teamed up with London-listed specialty materials developer Johnson Matthey, Harvard University and Boston-based quantum hardware specialist QuEra, in work overseen by the U.S. government's Advanced Research Projects Agency–Energy (ARPA-E), to discover novel catalysts that would reduce reliance on scarce metals like iridium — which is currently critical in the production of hydrogen.

Asked why Phasecraft is focusing on energy rather than sectors that might seem more lucrative, Montanaro said the returns were there too.

"When you get this right, the commercial returns are real: more efficient energy networks mean lower operating costs and more value from existing infrastructure," he said.

#2760. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2757. Observations after Sixers survive Bucks' late surge, reach 14-3

Observations after Sixers survive Bucks' late surge, reach 14-3 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers survived the Bucks' late surge and reached 14-3 on the season Saturday afternoon with a 132-129 win at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Tyler Herro nearly sent the game to overtime with a buzzer-beating three-pointer, but replays showed that he released his shot late.

Herro had a massive performance for the 1-16 Bucks, posting a career-high 50 points on 15-for-23 shooting, 11 rebounds and seven assists.

Joel Embiid had 26 points, a season-high 17 rebounds and nine assists for the Sixers.

LeBron James (21 points), Tyrese Maxey (17 points) and Anfernee Simons (18) were among the seven Sixers to score in double figures.

The Sixers were down Jaylen Brown (left hamstring strain), Dean Wade (left hamstring tightness/soreness), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear).

Next up for the Sixers is a Monday night meeting with the Trail Blazers. Here are observations on their victory over the Bucks:

Maxey returned to the Sixers' starting five after missing the team's comeback win Wednesday over the Magic with right foot soreness. He was joined by VJ Edgecombe, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, James and Embiid.

James threw down a dunk on the day's first play. Embiid then dished to a cutting Edgecombe for a layup that put the Sixers up 4-0.

The Bucks quickly found a foothold in the game.

Embiid missed his first four shots, including two that were swatted by Myles Turner. Maxey began 1 for 5 from the floor. Herro canned a three-pointer to give Milwaukee a 15-8 lead.

The Sixers entered the game with a minus-13.2 net rating in first quarters, which ranked 28th in the NBA. They faced a 30-29 deficit after Saturday's first period.

Even with Maxey available again, Labaron Philon Jr. deservingly stayed in the Sixers' rotation following an excellent outing in Orlando.

He scored two points in six first-half minutes. The 20-year-old made a rookie mistake at the tail end of the first quarter, fouling Herro on a three-point attempt.

Dominick Barlow checked in for his first action of the season with 2:51 remaining in the first quarter. He'd been sidelined for the Sixers' first 16 games because of a lower back stress reaction.

Barlow scored his first points early in the second quarter when he tipped in a Maxey miss. He then teamed up with James in crowd-pleasing style, sprinting ahead of the pack on a fast break and flushing home the 22-time All-Star's long-distance lob.

The afternoon was not devoid of rust for Barlow. Shortly after that alley-oop, he grabbed an offensive rebound, dribbled out to the perimeter and fired an open three-pointer … which he air balled short.

To Barlow's credit, he took another three early in the fourth quarter and drained it. He logged 11 minutes and was quite productive, tallying 12 points on 5-for-6 shooting and four rebounds.

"It felt great," Barlow said of his three. "Got to shoot the ball with confidence. … I've obviously got to get a game feel; I've been missing that. But I worked too hard (in the offseason) to not shoot the ball when I'm open."

Kyle Kuzma hit a three late in the second quarter to build the Bucks' lead to 59-52. After Sixers head coach Nick Nurse's timeout, the team closed the first half on a 8-0 run featuring an Embiid dunk.

The Sixers went just 3 for 16 from long range in the first half, but their low-turnover ways helped mitigate those shooting struggles.

They committed a single turnover in the first half and finished with four giveaways. That's tied for their lowest ever on record in a single game. The Sixers have the NBA's lowest turnover percentage outside of garbage time, according to Cleaning the Glass.

"No matter who you're playing against, teams try to turn you over, they try to speed you up," James said. "Us taking care of the ball, that's a lot of the reason why we won this game."

The Sixers' shooters were bound to start sinking some open looks eventually. Simons, Caldwell-Pope and Edgecombe all made threes late in the third quarter and the team's lead grew as high as 13 points.

Thanks largely to Herro's individuals exploits, the Bucks stayed competitive. AJ Green buried a corner three to cut Milwaukee's deficit to 106-103 with 8:10 to go in the game.

James sought out his shot much more following the Sixers' timeout and did so with great success. He scored four buckets inside over the next four minutes, including a big slam that put the Sixers up 121-112.

It appeared the main questions left were whether Embiid could reach a triple-double and just how many points Herro would rack up.

However, the Bucks forced the Sixers to work down the stretch. Herro got to the 50-point milestone and trimmed the Sixers' lead to two points when he converted a layup with 19.2 seconds left.

The Sixers safely navigated to the final buzzer by the slimmest of margins. Embiid made four late free throws and everyone on the Sixers' sideline was relieved to see that Herro hadn't quite released his jumper in time.

"I try not to complain too much about any win in this league," Nurse said. "The message is we've got to finish a little better."

#2756. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2753. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.
