#2751. 4 things we learned from the Giants’ 23-18 victory over the Jaguars

Sunday was the day the New York Giants' Cinderella season carriage was going to turn back into the pumpkin of past season failures. That might yet happen – the road ahead is filled with difficult opponents. Today, though, the Giants for the first time established themselves as serious playoff contenders. They not only hung with the previously 7-2 Jacksonville Jaguars, they actually dominated a good part of the game. Things got dicey in the second half as the Jaguars started to move the ball and tighten up their defense, but the Giants held on for a 23-18 victory that solidified their hold on first place (on a day when the Cowboys and Eagles both won) and perhaps more importantly, suggested that they might be more than just a competent team that can squeeze out victories over the NFL's bottom-dwellers. The NFL, which gambled that the Giants were for real by flexing their game into the national 4:30 window, won their bet with a thrilling game. What did we learn from the Giants' 23-18 win over Jacksonville?

There's been evidence of this since the start of the season, when Dennard Wilson's aggressive approach proved a welcome change from the passive Shane Bowen defense in the Giants' first game against Dallas. Still, most Giants games have been against poor to middling offensive teams, and when they faced a good offense, the Rams ran roughshod over them. Today it was the Jaguars' No. 5 scoring offense in the NFL, one that had put 30 or more points up 6 times already this season. The 18 points they scored against the Giants today was their second lowest of the season, trailing only the 12 points they scored in Denver (which has one of the NFL's best defenses). You have to credit the D as a whole. Trevor Lawrence did manage to put up 301 passing yards, but he only passed for 1 TD and was intercepted twice. Furthermore, the Jags' potent running game only had 77 yards, though admittedly star running back Bhayshul Tuten left with an injury mid-game.

The main reasons that was true today were:

Carter has been having what by some metrics would be considered a breakout season, ranking high in a number of pass rush metrics such as pass rush win rate and time to pressure. What has been lacking are sacks. Today, though, Carter sacked Lawrence twice and hit or at least pressured him a handful of other times. Carter's quickness and agility getting around blocks is other-worldly, and today he was finally rewarded for the work he's been doing. Forget the stats, when J.J. Watt seems impressed, that's good enough for me.

Part of the reason Carter was effective was that he wasn't alone. Kayvon Thobodeaux, as usual, did not get any sacks, but he was getting pressure from the other side as well (in addition to having a strong game against the run and intercepting a 2-point conversion attempt), and D.J. Reader was also getting pressure inside and had a sack of his own. There would have been more, but Lawrence is a pretty mobile QB for a guy his size, and the Giants had him running around a lot trying to escape and find someone downfield. The good thing is that the Giants did it without blitzing as much as they have in some recent games.

Part of the reason Lawrence had a long time to throw today (his longest of the season) was that the Giants did a pretty good job, all things considered, against the Jaguars' potent receiving corps. Parker Washington, who has become one of the NFL's best receivers, had 103 yards today, but that was on 17 targets, of which he only caught 8. Likewise, Jakobi Meyers had 85 yards, but again, on 10 targets of which he only caught four.

Much of that was due to Paulson Adebo. The Giants' big free agent signing last year, a disappointment then and relegated to the bench most of this year after being injured in Game 1, Adebo played perhaps his best game as a Giant. He held Washington to zero catches on three targets and Meyers to one 23-yard catch in five targets. He broke up four passes and also had an interception . Adebo entered the game after Greg Newsome left with an injury in the first quarter. If he is finally returning to his form of a couple of years ago when he was a Saint, that could be huge for the Giants, who will face some potent passing offenses in the coming weeks (he writes while watching Brock Purdy carve up the Vikings defense in the first half on Sunday Night Football).

Meanwhile, Deonte Banks continues to play great ball. Banks only gave up two receptions in 10 targets for 21 yards and had his second interception of the season. To me, this was his best game of the season, even though he had the game-saving pick-6 against Arizona. It is such a remarkable development after watching him languish the past two seasons. Part of Lawrence's problem tonight was that he couldn't find open receivers. If this continues this could be the Giants' best secondary since their 2022 playoff season.

In their first nine games, the Giants had five of them in which they gave up 163, 123, 202, 175, and 105 rushing yards. Today they gave up only 77 yards to a Jacksonville team that had rushed for over 100 yards in each of its last four games. The big news pre-game was that Darius Alexander was not active. That's quite a fall from grace for the former high third round draft pick who had given the Giants a little interior pass rush but who had been a liability against the run. In his place the Giants played Bobby Jamison-Travis, who's become a regular part of the rotation, and C.J. Okoye, who had missed the past three games. It's worth watching going forward to see how IDL snaps are distributed and whether the Giants are able to hold up against formidable rushing teams that are coming up such as the 49ers and Lions.

It can be frustrating to watch this Giants offense sometimes. J.J. McCarthy looked good early on, but he went downhill as the game progressed. To Matt Nagy's credit, the Giants finally dialed up some downfield passes for McCarthy today. Unfortunately, he was pretty awful on those passes – 0 for 5 beyond 20 yards, with most of them sailing well beyond the receiver. The Giants might have put up more than 30 if he'd had better touch downfield. He was pretty good on downfield passes as a Viking, so let's hope that today was just an aberration, because adding a downfield threat to their offense could be the difference between hanging with the good teams or being steamrolled by them.

The problem is that they need for J.J. to be good, because their rushing game has been suspect. The Giants do not have good run blockers, especially on the interior offensive line. John Harbaugh and Greg Roman have been searching all season for a bellcow to help their offense run at maximum efficiency. One problem is that all their running backs fumble. Another problem is that they tend to get injured. Another problem is that none of them are elite running backs even when they are at their best. Cam Skattebo got the call again tonight with Tyrone Tracy still injured…and he fumbled again tonight at a critical juncture. With the Giants already up 20-6, with a first down at the Jaguars' 33 and 4:01 left in the third quarter, once again Skattebo was stripped, his fifth of the season. Only a tackle by of all people J.J. McCarthy kept Antonio Johnson from returning it for a TD.

That was really a turning point in the game. It went from a solid, bordering on easy, victory for the Giants to a nail-biter. Jacksonville got the momentum, and by the 3:43 mark in the fourth quarter, Cam Little's field goal had gotten the Jaguars to within 23-18. Be honest – did you think the Giants were going to lose at that point?

I did. Then something remarkable happened. After Skattebo ran for 3 yards, McCarthy completed a 19-yard pass to Malachi Fields to get the Giants to the Jacksonville 43 yard line with 3:02 left. Then Skattebo ran for 5 yards. Then he ran for another 5 yards and a first down. Then the Giants were able to just run the clock to zeros with McCarthy kneel-downs and keepers. I don't think we've seen that since the opening game against Dallas. Overall the Giants finished with 77 rushing yards, but 33 of those were by McCarthy, basically on scrambles (he lost yardage on a couple of designed runs).

The question is why the Giants running game was finally able to run out the clock, and whether they can finally start to do that earlier in the game. They are not a terrible running team – entering today they were middle of the pack in team rushing yards. But they are eighth-worst in yards per carry. And we know that they want to be a running team. Today they had 3.1 yards per carry, pretty pedestrian.

Yet in the end they managed to run out the clock. So I can't decide whether they are or are not a bad running team. Hence the strikethrough on the section title above. Let's see how the rest of the season progresses.

#2750. James sees a lot to like — and improve — for East-leading Sixers

James sees a lot to like — and improve — for East-leading Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James likes to refer to the NBA season as a "marathon."

A few miles in, the Sixers lead the Eastern Conference pack.

With 17 regular-season contests down of 82, the team sits at 14-3, which is 2.5 games ahead of the second-seeded Celtics. The Sixers squeaked out their latest win, a 132-129 decision Saturday over the cellar-dwelling Bucks, but the bottom line is they've become accustomed to finding many routes to victory.

"We want to build habits," James said. "We want to try to take steps forward every game that we get the opportunity. I know sometimes it doesn't happen that way, but we want to do it.

"We want to strive to be as great as we can offensively, defensively and on special teams. A win is a win, but there's ways to win and ways to try to continue to build habits over the course of a marathon."

All sorts of Sixers games have gone down to the wire and they've won every single one.

The team is an incredible 12-0 in clutch situations, defined as games with a point differential of five or under in the final five minutes. The Sixers rank third in the NBA in clutch offensive rating (140.3) and third in clutch net rating (plus-42.0).

"It's good to build that part of our game," James said. "Obviously, we wanted to close the game out a lot better. But a team like that, we know they're going to be scrappy. They're going to play as hard as they can for 48 minutes. … We can learn from that."

James was the centerpiece of the Sixers' fourth-quarter offense Saturday. He scored 13 of his 21 points between the 7:46 and 3:13 mark of the fourth.

The four-time MVP got two buckets on no-nonsense drives and another on an after-timeout isolation hoop over rookie Brayden Burries. James also scored three times on a designed Horns action where he brought the ball up, used Tyrese Maxey's back screen at the elbow and caused serious problems for Milwaukee in the paint.

"We continue to add things to the half-court offense and also to the late-game package," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "That's something we added a few game ago. It's one of those things where, especially if we're in the bonus, we've got some big bodies that can cut down the lane and have feel for timing. It was working. Just keep going to it."

James has grown in comfort with Nurse's coaching style. He knows the Sixers expect him to be a de facto coach on the floor, too.

"I'm just trying to be there for the team in any capacity they want," he said. "Just trying to be an extension of his calls and his commands out there on the floor. Trying to make his life and his job a little bit easier as well.

"But it's been great, not only with him but the whole coaching staff. They've welcomed me with open arms and allowed me to kind of be the voice for the team every day, and take that responsibility."

After a tremendous first impression defensively and some subpar offensive nights, the Sixers' numbers have recently evened out.

The team is 12th in offensive rating outside of garbage time and 12th in defensive rating, per Cleaning the Glass. In essence, the Sixers have been a bit above average on both ends of the floor and downright brilliant in the closing minutes.

It's all added up to an early lead in a very long race.

"I think we've bought into the pass," James said. "We have so many guys that can score the ball at elite levels. … In order for us to be a super-complete team, I think the power of the pass is very infectious. And it's also key for our other guys, to make sure they feel in a rhythm and they feel confident. We've done that from Day 1 and I think it's gotten better and better. … And I think our defense has been really good for the majority of the season.

"(Today) it wasn't so great, but we've been very good defensively throughout the course of the season. … I don't want to say there's anything bad. There's so many things for us to do better for sure. There's games where we can defensive rebound a lot better and there's games where we can not turn the ball over (as much). And there's games where we can be a little more phsyical defensively at the point of attack like (today).

"I wouldn't say anything has been bad, though. That's a good thing. We want to continue to have room for improvement and we don't want to be great in November. That would be weird."

#2749. ‘More than just a game’: How GTA VI’s blockbuster launch fuels a booming content economy

Grand Theft Auto VI has exploded out of the gate after a 13-year wait, drawing millions of buyers while fueling a parallel economy of livestreams, clips and other content built around people watching the game as much as playing it.

GTA VI has delivered a blockbuster debut, with about 19.1 million copies sold — including preorders — by Thursday morning, representing roughly $1.84 billion in sales, according to Sensor Tower. The GTA franchise itself has sold more than 470 million units worldwide.

Take-Two Interactive, which owns the studio behind the game, has not yet disclosed a profit figure for the title.

Ross Gerber, president and CEO of Gerber Kawasaki Wealth & Investment Management, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" that he sees further upside for Take-Two, which his firm holds in its portfolio.

"It's crazy good. I've never seen anything like it," Gerber said, hailing the title as "more than just a game" and pointing to the franchise's potential to expand into film and television.

But that launch is also spawning a second economy around the enormous global hype and demand for GTA VI content.

Viewers watched 125.5 million hours of the title's content in its first 24 hours across platforms including Twitch, YouTube and Kick, according to Streams Charts.

YouTube creator Darren "IShowSpeed" Watkins Jr. led streamers with 4.47 million hours viewed across YouTube and Twitch. Separately, Twitch tracker CastAccord recorded about two million hours watched over roughly 36 hours of streamer Kai Cenat's launch broadcast.

Both creators had built anticipation with trailers for their own GTA VI coverage. As of Nov. 19, IShowSpeed's trailer had drawn 3.7 million YouTube views, while Cenat's had amassed 2.7 million.

According to Forbes, IShowSpeed and Cenat are estimated to earn about $30 million and $8.5 million a year, respectively, from their broader creator businesses.

Rockstar Games, the studio behind the franchise, has also moved to capitalize on the surge in viewer interest, partnering with YouTube on a 24-hour launch broadcast and introducing themed gifts that viewers could send during select streams.

Outside creators and agencies are also trying to capture the surge in demand for Grand Theft Auto content.

Agency Clipped & Shipped is offering a $10,000 prize pool to editors who can drive audiences to clips from Kai Cenat's streams, with qualifying submissions distributed across YouTube Shorts, TikTok, Instagram and X.

A separate campaign offered $1 per 1,000 views for clips of streamer KreekCraft, with individual submissions eligible to earn up to $400.

Those audiences could translate into thousands of dollars in creator revenue.

Using AIR Media-Tech's U.S. YouTube Shorts benchmark, 1.5 million eligible engaged views could generate about $490, while 5.1 million would yield roughly $1,670.

The two Vice City Dispatch videos could generate about $3,840 based on higher benchmark rates for long-form content. The channels' actual earnings could not be independently verified.

Moreover, the content economy is becoming increasingly automated.

On X, users are pitching artificial intelligence-assisted clipping operations that scan livestreams for spikes in chat activity, automatically cut highlights and distribute them across multiple accounts.

One user claimed an operation generated $21,000 overnight from 2.7 million views, equivalent to about $7.78 per 1,000 reported views.

The earnings claims could not be independently verified. X bases payouts on qualified impressions from Premium users, which are not reflected in public view counts.

And appetite for more content — and more revenue — may only be starting.

Wedbush analyst Alicia Reese told CNBC that in-game purchases could generate "years of high-margin recurring revenue" for Take-Two, which she believes investors have yet to fully price in.

She expects the console launch to be followed by a PC release and then an online multiplayer mode, mirroring the rollout of the previous title in the franchise.

Rockstar's launch announcement listed PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series X and S, with no timetable for either potential follow-on. GTA VI has also picked up eight nominations for the Game Awards, including game of the year, best game direction, narrative, art direction, score and music, audio design, action/adventure and performance.

#2748. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2745. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

#2744. Tata Steel to buy $1.67 billion stake in India’s largest steel pipe maker; shares plunge nearly 5%

Tata Steel, one of the world's leading steel producers, on Monday said it will buy a 26.4% stake in India's largest structural steel pipe and tube maker, APL Apollo, for 155.1 billion rupees ($1.67 billion).

The acquisition will boost Tata Steel's downstream integration to around 53% from 35%, the company said in a stock exchange filing. That will lead to more value addition, improved product mix, and greater exposure to higher-margin downstream segments, it said.

Shares of Tata Steel were nearly 5% lower on Monday, while APL Apollo shares tanked over 10% in early trade.

APL Apollo said after the deal it plans to more than double the downstream capacity to 15.5 million tons from the current 7.5 million tons, making it a leading global downstream steel firm.

This capacity addition will be completed by 2035 and would include around 9 million tons per annum of steel tubes and around 6.5 million tons per annum of color-coated products, as per Tata Steel.

Tata Steel will acquire a stake from existing shareholders of APL Apollo for 2,100 rupees per share and will ​also make an open offer to acquire an additional 26% ‌stake for 2,301.40 rupees a piece.

If the open offer is successful, Tata Steel would get a controlling stake of 52.38% in APL Apollo. World's second-largest steel maker ArcelorMittal was also reportedly in the fray to acquire APL Apollo.

The acquisition of APL Apollo would "create a downstream powerhouse for the future" that will serve the growing Indian market, T V Narendran, chief executive and managing director of Tata Steel, said in the release.

Tata Steel, which has an annual crude steel capacity of over 35 million tons per annum, is one of the world's most geographically diversified steel producers with presence across India, Europe and Thailand.

#2741. This Anthropic proxy play is getting traders' attention – here's where they see it going

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. Currently, 14% of the fund's assets are tied to Anthropic, according to the company's website.Options volume on the fund jumped to more than 10x the 30-day average Monday, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Of the 42,300 contracts traded by midday, 41,800 were calls, with just 141 puts likely opened by buyers.

Just shy of $5.5 million in options premium on DXYZ traded on Monday, with almost all of it tied to calls, but just marginally more spent buying than selling calls, according to SpotGamma. The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. Since then, the fund has barely moved, trading around $26.There are other bullish signs surrounding Anthropic's IPO in the derivatives markets. Current prices for perpetual futures on the blockchain exchange Hyperliquid are implying an opening price of $98, a big pop from the $70-$78 price range quoted in the company's amended S1 filing. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

#2740. Anthropic’s record IPO collides with tech’s worst week on Wall Street since 2020

Startup investor Ben Narasin is so jazzed about Anthropic's blockbuster IPO that he's planning a party later this month at his Silicon Valley home. Sake, vodka and Krug Champagne will be served. Each attendee will be given two jars of caviar.

"This is a celebration of the most material, significant, and mind-blowingly awesome event that has ever happened in venture capital," Narasin, founding partner of Tenacity Venture Capital, told CNBC in an interview, adding that he didn't even invest in the company.

Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO and an additional $26 billion from tech giants that bought at the offer price. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, lifting Anthropic's market cap to $1.8 trillion and providing a much-needed windfall for Silicon Valley's startup ecosystem.

It was quite the split-screen moment on Wall Street, which spent the week in an AI-driven tailspin. Led by Nvidia's 23% plunge, which marked the stock's sharpest weekly pullback in 18 years, the Nasdaq slid 10% over the past five trading days in its worst week since the early days of Covid in 2020.

Investors and analysts spent the week debating whether Anthropic will trade alongside AI hardware, as it's reliant on advanced chips, memory and data centers, or whether it will stand alone as the first public pure-play AI developer and a company that's achieved unrivaled growth in less than six years of existence.

That question will likely persist for some time, but Friday's market action showed that Wall Street is skeptical of Anthropic's ability to grow into its lofty valuation. At week's end, the Claude creator's $1.6 trillion fully diluted market cap put it behind just six U.S. companies: Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet, Nvidia, Amazon and SpaceX.

With the tech market in turmoil due to rising interest rates, raging anti-AI sentiment and Nvidia's C-suite crisis, Anthropic's IPO was part exuberance, part relief for an industry hungry for exits.

After venture-backed IPOs yielded $73 billion in proceeds in 2021, the amount raised in the next four years totaled just $39 billion, according to data from University of Florida emeritus professor Jay Ritter. In 2021, 126 technology companies went public, compared with 34 in 2025.

Meanwhile, the private market has continued to balloon, with companies raising ever-larger sums at sky-high valuations without the pressures of quarterly earnings. Many startups that had hoped to go public early in the decade fell out of favor in the AI boom. They experienced hefty job cuts, down rounds and acquisitions for pennies on the dollar.

"There was a time in Silicon Valley not that long ago where the prevailing wisdom was the default path should be avoiding going public for as long as possible," said Somesh Dash, a general partner at venture firm IVP. The view was, "it's too long, it's too hard, it's not worth it," he said.

Anthropic is very young compared to some of those companies, but it was a huge collector of private capital. Investors poured $141 billion into the AI lab as of September, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

For a few Silicon Valley firms that contributed to those funding rounds, the returns are unlike anything they've ever seen. At the IPO price, Menlo Ventures and Lightspeed Venture Partners each owned about $25 billion worth of Anthropic stock. Bessemer Venture Partners owned a stake worth roughly $10 billion, partner Byron Deeter said.

Matt Murphy, a partner at Menlo, told CNBC's David Faber on Thursday that his firm has more than five limited partners "that we delivered over $1 billion of returns to."

"At the end of the day, that's really our job, is to get dollars back into foundations and endowments," Murphy said. "There's a lot of pride in being able to do that."

Bessemer's Deeter was also jubilant on Thursday, posting on X that, in addition to being "the largest bet in Bessemer's history," the IPO "is a punctuation mark in the history of both humanity and computation."

That's quite a change from a few years earlier, when Anthropic was hardly generating revenue, and success was far from guaranteed. In 2023, Anthropic's Claude 2 was struggling to keep pace with models from Google and OpenAI. In April 2024, investors at Bessemer wrote in a memo that if Anthropic missed its own targets, there was "a good chance that we lose all or part of our money."

Guru Chahal at Lightspeed told CNBC that Anthropic was clearly the team taking a more measured approach to AI. The company looked beyond Nvidia's dominant AI chips, choosing to use alternatives from Amazon and Google as well. Anthropic said it generated $1.72 in revenue per dollar spent on computing power in the third quarter, up from 68 cents a year earlier.

"It is true that it required lots of capital, but when you dig in and you realize that capital is going toward compute, and this is the team that's positioned to use that compute in the most effective way, then we could connect those dots," Chahal said. He added that those metrics showed it was "going to be a very high-quality business."

Anthropic isn't the only big win for VCs this year.

Five months before Anthropic's Nasdaq debut, SpaceX hit the market in what, until this week, was by far the largest IPO in history. SpaceX, which Elon Musk founded in 2002, produced outsized returns for firms including Sequoia and Andreessen Horowitz.

SpaceX and Anthropic alone will make 2026 a monster year for the venture industry, easily exceeding any other year in IPO proceeds. And investors in OpenAI hope appetite remains strong for trillion-dollar companies next year, as the ChatGPT maker is looking to 2027 for a potential offering.

IVP's Dash said that in his AI portfolio, "a lot of companies are starting IPO prep" even if it's only "year four, year five, year six since inception." He hopes to see at least half a dozen companies go public at valuations north of $10 billion each in the second half of 2027.

Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia has left the chip giant without a clear leader at a time when the company already faced heightened concerns about a slowdown in spending. Nvidia is a key investor in Anthropic and OpenAI and a critical supplier and financing partner for the entire AI industry, which is getting hammered across the board.

In January, Democrats will take control of the House and Senate after this month's midterm elections, which included the passage of numerous ballot measures that could stymie data center development. Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner predicted after the election that market dynamics would push Anthropic to price toward the lower end of its range.

Narasin said he wasn't even sure the company would get that far.

"I was concerned they'd delay," Narasin said.

That makes the outcome that much more impressive, he said, after Anthropic priced above its expected range and upsized the offering.

"This will be bedtime stories for years for future VCs," Narasin said. "There was a company that was created, backed by angels and later venture" that in a few short years "became the single biggest IPO of all history."

— CNBC's Ari Levy contributed to this report.

#2739. Here is where the Seahawks are in the NFC playoff picture after Sunday night

The Seattle Seahawks are realistically out of the NFC West race after the San Francisco 49ers walloped the Minnesota Vikings 35-10 in Mexico City. 8. Seattle Seahawks (6-4, 2-1 NFCW, 2-3 NFC)

12. Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-7, 1-0 vs ATL+NO)

14. New Orleans Saints (3-7, 0-1 vs TB+ATL, 1-5 NFC)

#2737. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2736. How Asian countries are looking to prop up markets as AI downturn sinks stocks

Market regulators in South Korea, China and Taiwan have introduced new measures aimed at supporting markets, as a tech-fueled sell-off in the region spills into another week.

On Sunday, South Korea's finance ministry said it would prepare its securities-market stabilization fund for immediate activation. The fund, reportedly established in 2020 during the Covid-19 pandemic by financial firms and other institutions seeks to support markets during sharp downturns and is valued at 10.76 trillion won (nearly $8 billion).

The government also plans to relax listed companies' daily share-buyback order limits for three months, starting Nov. 24 and start a "special intensive monitoring period" to prevent illegal short-selling. Regulators will also track margin lending and forced liquidations.

South Korean stocks have seen wild swings over 2026 as investors piled into, then fled from its two tech heavyweights, Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix, both critical companies in the AI supply chain. The benchmark Kospi index is deep in bear territory, down more than 46% from its June high.

The ministry said that it "assessed that volatility in domestic and international stock markets has significantly increased recently due to internal and external uncertainties surrounding the global AI and semiconductor industries, as well as U.S. interest rate hikes," according to a Google translation of the release in Korean.

However, Seoul also said that the mid-to-long-term growth expectations for the AI industry remain intact, and that "excessive anxiety is not a concern."

Taiwan, which is also exposed to the AI industry through Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company and other tech firms, has also moved to rein in market volatility.

Taiwan's Financial Supervisory Commission announced Friday that short selling below the closing price was "completely prohibited," while making it an exception if used for hedging purposes by securities and futures firms.

"Investors must not sell listed (OTC) stocks or Taiwan Depositary Receipts at a price lower than the closing price of the previous trading day," the FSC said, according to a Google translation of the statement in Chinese.

Margin requirements for short selling will also be increased, and applications from securities firms to issue warrants for listing will be suspended, as the regulator aims to curb speculative activity.

In a similar move to Seoul, Taipei encouraged firms to buy back stocks, adding that the purchases will be included in the companies' environmental, social and governance scores if completed between the fourth quarter of 2026 and the first quarter of 2027.

The island's stock market is heavily dominated by its tech industry, most notably, chip manufacturing giant TSMC. The stock fell 15% last week, while the index dropped 17.5%.

While South Korea and Taiwan are looking to curb speculative trading to arrest the slide in their markets, Beijing is looking to encourage more leverage-based buying.

The Shanghai Stock Exchange said on Sunday that exchanges in Shanghai, Shenzhen and Beijing will cut the minimum margin ratio for margin-financed stock purchases to 80% from 100%, starting Nov. 30. That means investors will be able to borrow more for their stock market bets.

The Shanghai and Shenzhen exchanges also announced fee cuts, while Shenzhen proposed a market-making mechanism for its ChiNext board. The steps will help inject more liquidity into the market that has also born the brunt of the souring in tech and AI trade, with the benchmark CSI 300 index closing lower for 14 of the past 15 weeks.

Seoul's markets led losses in the region, with the Kospi down over 3.26%. Smaller losses were seen in mainland China's CSI 300, which declined 0.71%. The Taiwan Weighted Index, however, reversed earlier losses to gain over 2% on Monday.

#2735. The 76ers are fun again, so it's the return of Roob's 76ers Stats!

The 76ers are fun again, so it's the return of Roob's 76ers Stats! originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

This 76ers team is a heck of a lot of fun to watch and fun basketball means fun stats!

At 13-3, the 76ers are exactly a month into the 5th-best start in franchise history and best since they were 14-2 in 2000-01. Which turned out to be a pretty good year. They were 15-1 in 1949-50 and 1966-67 and 14-2 in 1981-82.

To help everybody get through this interminable two-day gap between 76ers games, here's our first 2026-27 installment of Roob's 76ers Stats, with a tip of the hat to Stathead.

→ The 76ers have been out-rebounded by 34 through 16 games (719-685) and their 13-3 record matches the best in NBA history by a team that's been out-rebounded after 16 games. Three other teams have been 13-3 despite getting out-rebounded, most recently the 2013-14 Miami Heat. The others are the 1979-80 Bucks and 2012-2013 Spurs. The 76ers are an NBA-best 6-3 this year when they've been out-rebounded (which means they're 7-0 when they have a rebounding edge).

→ In the win over Orlando Thursday, V.J. Edgecombe had 24 points, eight rebounds, six assists and two steals, and Joel Embiid had 26 points, six rebounds, seven assists and two steals. It was the first time two 76ers had at least 24 points, six rebounds and assists and two steals in a game in 53 years. In a win over the Celtics at the Spectrum in 1973, Fred Carter had 31 points, six rebounds, six assists and seven steals and George McGinniss had 28 points, 16 rebounds, eight assists and three steals.

→ Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, a career 37 percent shooter from 3, is shooting 44 percent so far. Anfernee Simons, a career 38 percent shooter from 3, is shooting 41 percent. Jabari Walker, a career 33 percent 3-point shooter, is at 42 percent. Dean Wade, a career 37 percent shooter, is at 41 percent. Jaylen Brown, a career 36 percent shooter from range, is at 39 percent. The 76ers are shooting just 36 percent as a team from the stripe because James and Embiid have been so bad, but everybody else is off to career-best starts from 3-point range.

→ The 76ers are 7-1 on the road, equalling their best road record ever after eight games. They were also 7-1 in 1980-81, 1981-82 and 1982-83. The 1981 team had the best road start in franchise history, going 12-1 before losing its second road game.

→ With 15.7 points, 6.8 assists and 6.8 rebounds per game, LeBron James is only the fifth 76er ever to average at least 15 ½ points and 6 ½ assists and rebounds through 16 games. The others are some familiar names: Wilt Chamberlain in 1967, Charles Barkley in 1987, Ben Simmons in 2018 and James Harden in 2023.

→ The 76ers already have more wins this year after trailing by 10 points through the first quarter than all but two full seasons since 1950. They had four in 1985-86 and again in 2022-23. The Hawks also have three wins this year when trailing by double digits after the first quarter, but they're 3-4 and the 76ers are 3-2. So they actually have a winning record when they're down by double digits going into the second quarter.

→ With Jaylen Brown (22.6, 6.6), Joel Embiid (20.0, 8.3), LeBron James (15.7, 6.8) and V.J. Edgecombe (13.6, 6.5), the 76ers are the only NBA team with four players averaging 6 ½ rebounds and 13 points. The last time they had four players in a season who averaged 13 points and 6 ½ rebounds was 1961-62 when they were the Syracuse Nationals, with Dave Gambee (16.7, 7.9), Hal Greer (22.8, 7.4), Red Kerr (16.3, 14.7), Dolph Schayes (14.7, 7.8) and Lee Shaffer (16.9, 6.8).

→ Edgecombe's 6.5 rebounds per game are 2nd-most among shooting guards behind only the Grizzlies' Cedric Coward, who is at 8.5. Edgecombe's 6.5 is also 2nd-highest ever by a 76ers shooting guard behind only Greer's 7.4 during that 1961-62 season. Edgecombe averaged 5.6 rebounds as a rookie and his 5.8 career average is highest by a shooting guard who spent more than one year with the 76ers. Greer is second at 5.3. Among all NBA players who've played at least 90 games, Edgecombe's 5.8 average is 11th-highest all-time among shooting guards (although Stathead includes some swing guard-forwards in their data).

→ Joel Embiid's 14.6 shooting percentage from 3 is worst in NBA history after 16 games (minimum 40 attempts). Embiid is 6-for-41 from the stripe this year. The previous 3-point shooting low 16 games into a season belonged to Cedric Ceballos of the Lakers in 1995-96 and the 76ers' Evan Turner in 2013-14. Both were 6-for-43 for 16.3 percent.

→ LeBron James is at 22.2 percent from 3 on 10-for-45. The Sixers are the first NBA team in 12 years with two players under 23 percent after 16 games with a minimum of 40 attempts. In 2014 Tony Wroten was 10-for-50 (10 percent) and Turner was 6-for-43 (16.3 percent).

Kirk Cousins of the Minnesota Vikings is hit by Nick Bosa of the San Francisco 49ers. (Photo by Manuel Velasquez/Getty Images)

Carson Wentz is in for the Vikings now, and Brock Purdy's day is done, too, as we hit the two mintue warning.

The Vikings got all the way to the SF 20, but their attempt on fourth down was stopped just a few yards short of the line to gain.

#2732. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2731. Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton warns Americans of 'bumps in the road' after tech outages

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton warned Americans to prepare for "bumps in the road" after a set of damaging technology outages rocked air travel and systems like Microsoft Outlook.

Government officials have largely dismissed the prospects that the outages, which also slowed bank transactions, are linked to a coordinated or artificial intelligence cyberattack.

Clayton, however, said on CNN's "State of the Union" that outages may continue as companies and the government upgrade their systems to adapt to the AI age.

"The American people should be prepared for a great deal of investment in upgrading our systems; they should be prepared for investment in cybersecurity, and they should be prepared that any time there is a step change in technology, a step change in both compute and applications, there will be bumps in the road," Clayton said.

"You prepare by knowing that systems are going to be upgraded; they may be interrupted for a period of time and that companies and government are dealing with this on the front foot," he said.

Clayton's warning came as the U.S. prepares to celebrate Thanksgiving on Thursday, one of the busiest travel weeks of the year. A technology outage prompted a nationwide ground stop just over a week ago, which lasted for hours.

The Federal Aviation Administration said router upgrades caused a flood of data that led to the ground stop, and that there is no indication of a cyberattack.

Separately, Microsoft said the Outlook outage was caused by "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade."

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent delivered a similar message on CBS' "Face the Nation."

Asked if Americans should be concerned about the security of their financial institutions, Bessent said: "No, they shouldn't."

"The patches may cause a glitch," he said, referring to measures banks are taking to safeguard from AI. "I think the American people should think of it as a vaccine; your arm may hurt, you may feel a little bad the next day, but it increases your long-term immunity and resiliency."

#2729. Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory

Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stretched their winning streak to four games and snagged a strong road win Wednesday night over the Magic.

They earned a 121-116 victory in Orlando to improve to 13-3 on the season. The Magic dropped to 6-8.

Joel Embiid had 26 points, seven assists, six rebounds, three blocks and two steals.

VJ Edgecombe posted a season-high 24 points, eight boards and six assists.

LeBron James scored 22 points and Anfernee Simons added 18.

Magic star Franz Wagner had 27 points, 11 assists and eight rebounds.

The Sixers had a long injury list in Orlando. Tyrese Maxey (right foot soreness), Jaylen Brown (left hamstring strain) and Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness) were among their players out. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse told reporters in Orlando that Brown will be re-evaluated when the team returns to Philadelphia.

Orlando was without star forward Paolo Banchero (right high ankle sprain).

The Sixers will start a three-game homestand when they face the Bucks on Saturday afternoon. Here are observations on their win over the Magic:

The Sixers' undermanned starting lineup was Edgecombe, Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, James and Embiid.

After scoring 37 points in the Sixers' win over the Nets, Embiid had a subpar start. He began 1 for 6 from the floor and committed a turnover that led to an Anthony Black dunk.

The Sixers' lineup looked exactly like a unit that hadn't built any chemistry yet. The team was not sharp early on either end of the floor.

Desmond Bane drained a long three-pointer to give Orlando a 27-12 advantage. Orlando made 11 of its first 14 field goals and led by as many as 17 points in the first quarter.

Labaron Philon Jr. and Justin Edwards joined the Sixers' rotation. So did two-way contract players Tacko Fall and Caleb Love.

Philon impressed in the first quarter, converting two floaters and a corner three. The rookie passed his previous NBA scoring high of five points and finished the night with 10 on 4-for-5 shooting.

Fall played 10 minutes and posted two points, three rebounds and three blocks. He slammed in a dunk and rejected two shots early in the second quarter. Credit to Fall for giving the Sixers a good backup center performance and doing his job as a rim-protecting ultra-big man.

Love entered in the second quarter and was ready to fire, hitting a catch-and-shoot three-pointer on his first touch.

The Sixers' zone defense also helped them eat into the Magic's lead. The zone seemed to disrupt Orlando's offensive rhythm and it was active enough to prevent the Magic from consistently creating high-quality looks.

Edgecombe's buzzer-beating mid-range jumper cut the Sixers' deficit to 69-64 after two quarters.

The Sixers pulled within a single point on multiple occasions in the third quarter, including on a Philon three late in the period. They trailed by five points after the third.

Fall continued to play well early in the fourth quarter. He deterred the Magic's drivers from getting all the way to the rim as the very large back line of the Sixers' zone defense and made eye-catching hustle plays on both sides of the ball.

The Sixers outscored Orlando by seven points on the night with Fall on the court. Soon after Embiid subbed back in, he evened the game at 104-all with two free throws. Jabari Walker swished a three to finally push the Sixers in front.

The Magic regained a late lead with a Jalen Suggs three. James then drove and kicked the ball out to Edgecombe, who nailed one of his customary clutch jumpers to put the Sixers back on top.

No matter the night-to-night personnel, the Sixers have been fantastic at the tail end of close games. They're now 11-0 in clutch situations, which the NBA defines as games within a five-point differential in the final five minutes.

#2727. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2726. U.S. probes Megaspeed, Alice Huang as new figure emerges in Nvidia chip case

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton said the U.S. is investigating Alice Huang and her former company, Megaspeed, as a report identified one of her associates as a key decision-maker at the Singapore-based company.

Alice Huang, with whom ousted Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang reportedly had a romantic relationship, was a key figure in Megaspeed International, a cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia linked to the fallout surrounding the leadership shake-up.

"There is an investigation ongoing into her firm and into her," Clayton told ABC on Sunday, adding that, "Nvidia chips, they are the jewel. They are the absolute top that everybody wants, that's why we're focusing on this."

Alice Huang's associate, Chinese national Li Ming, who is now out on bail in Singapore, was "deeply involved" in Megaspeed's activities and took part in major decisions, The Wall Street Journal reported earlier on Saturday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The Singapore charges against Li concern alleged false representations to Super Micro Computer, a U.S.-based firm with known links to chipmaker Nvidia, though Megaspeed is not named in those charges.

Last week, the Commerce and Justice departments said that the investigation into Megaspeed began in 2024 over potential export-control violations. Their joint statement said Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security and Singapore law enforcement interviewed Huang during an on-site check in June 2025.

The Justice Department later took the lead in the criminal investigation.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CBS on Sunday that the administration was seeking answers from Nvidia about its chip sales to China, including who oversaw compliance and which levels of the company knew about the activity.

Separately, The New York Times reported Sunday that Alice Huang was in contact with an officer of a top Chinese intelligence agency from spring 2025 through fall 2026, citing people briefed on U.S. intelligence reports.

Bloomberg reported last week that the Justice Department is investigating whether entities linked to Alice Huang helped smuggle Super Micro products to China.

Li faces five charges in Singapore, including two counts of fraud by false representation, along with allegations involving the operation of a company he controlled, unauthorized bank-account access and another company director's duties.

Prosecutors allege he falsely told Super Micro that the company would hold and lease the servers it purchased.

The Journal reported that Li is out on bail and that his case is ongoing. He was offered bail of S$1 million in March 2025. The Singapore charges do not name Megaspeed.

Earlier this month, Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said that police had found insufficient evidence to establish that Alice Huang committed offenses related to the possible inaccurate declarations under investigation.

The ministry said she was issued a stern warning for providing information that police assessed as knowingly inaccurate and for downplaying her role at Megaspeed. It also said Singapore had not investigated her for espionage.

Megaspeed has said it is a "legitimate and lawful business" and that Huang left the company in August 2025.

The scrutiny is set to intensify in Washington. Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and the company's general counsel, Tim Teter, have agreed to closed-door, transcribed interviews with three House committees on Dec. 1 and Dec. 2, respectively.

The committees said they want answers about Nvidia's role in allowing Chinese actors to access advanced chips and the involvement of a potential Chinese intelligence asset.

Speaking to CNBC's Squawk Box on Sunday U.S. time, Ryan Fedasiuk, a former advisor for U.S.-China Bilateral Affairs at the State Department, said that there was "still a lot we don't know" about the case.

He said lawmakers would likely focus on Nvidia's customer relationships and "who knew what at the company when."

Fedasiuk added that there was "a very good chance" investigators would find that people at Nvidia knew their products could be used for restricted purposes. He characterized the existing evidence as containing "multiple" smoking guns.

#2722. Yemen's Houthis claim attacks on Saudi oil refinery as Trump warns of new strikes next year

Yemen's Houthis, backed by Iran, said Sunday that they had targeted Rabigh oil refinery in Saudi Arabia, in the latest sign of worsening hostilities in the region, as Iranian forces seek to pressure the U.S. into accepting its terms.

The Houthis said they has directly hit the infrastructure, though the extent of the damage remains unclear, Reuters reported.

The Saudi-led coalition fighting the Houthis said earlier in the day that it had "intercepted and destroyed" a drone launched by the Houthis toward Saudi Arabia's western region, which included some of the country's critical energy refining and export infrastructure.

Rabigh, about 165 kilometers north of Jeddah on the kingdom's western Red Sea coast, hosts Petro Rabigh, a major oil refining and petrochemical complex, developed by Saudi Aramco and Japan's Sumitomo Chemical.

Saudi Arabia's Ministry of Interior did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

Gulf countries reported multiple attacks by Iran-linked forces over the weekend, even as the country's foreign minister said his government remains open to a negotiated end to the war. Bahrain authorities said an Iranian attack on a civilian industrial facility injured two people on Saturday.

The string of attacks mark the latest sign of escalation in the regional hostilities since the war between the U.S.-Israel and Iran began in late February, as negotiators have so far failed to agree on terms to end the monthslong war.

The conflict has drawn in countries in the Gulf region, disrupting energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz, which used to carry a fifth of the world's crude shipments prior to the war, pushing up global prices for oil and commodities.

The United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations reported another incident involving a tanker and military forces in the Gulf of Oman on Sunday, after two tankers were struck by unknown projectiles the day before. The maritime agency gave no further details about the incident, urging vessels to stay vigilant about the operational environment.

Tehran has indicated its intention to end the war with the U.S. through diplomacy, but President Donald Trump has dismissed Iranian proposals. Both sides have been in indirect communication via Qatar.

In an interview to Fox News on Saturday, Trump warned that he was inclined to order military strikes against Tehran next year, if the two countries failed to finalize an agreement. The president blamed the Iranian officials for stalling while escalating hostilities after the U.S. elections.

"They're more aggressive now than before the election," Trump said, describing Iranian officials as "professional tap dancers." "They talk all the time, all they do is talk," he added.

The president has previously said that Tehran wants a deal to end hostilities but not one that is "acceptable" to him. He has oscillated between favoring an agreement that would end the fighting and insisting that the U.S. does not need to rush into any deal and is not afraid of resuming more aggressive attacks.

The Iranian Deputy Foreign Minister Kazem Gharibabadi said Saturday after a two-hour meeting with visiting Qatari mediators that, "We are in contact with mediators, including the Qataris, both in person and virtually, and are holding the necessary discussions to see whether another opportunity can be given to diplomacy," according to Reuters.

International benchmark Brent futures inched 0.22% lower to $92.35 per barrel in early Asia trading hours, while WTI crude was down 0.19% at $83.25 a barrel.

#2721. NFL says Parker Washington didn't control ball until he was out of bounds

Sunday’s game between the Jaguars and Giants included a controversial decision in the fourth quarter on a play that looked like a touchdown catch by Jacksonville receiver Parker Washington.

The ruling on the field was that the catch was incomplete. Jaguars coach Liam Coen challenged the decision.

Replay review confirmed the ruling on the field, even though CBS rules analyst Gene Steratore said during the broadcast that he believed it was a catch.

After the game, pool reporter Pat Leonard spoke with NFL V.P. of instant replay Mark Butterworth.

“The ruling on the field was an incomplete pass,” Butterworth said. “First, to overturn a call, we need clear and obvious video evidence to overturn the on-field ruling. It was determined that the receiver did not gain full control of the ball until he was out of bounds.”

Butterworth confirmed that he was referring to the fact that, when Washington hit the ground, the ball was moving in Washington’s hands.

Still, movement of the ball is permitted. Washington objectively remained in control of the ball.

And here’s the real point. Movement doesn’t prevent clear and obvious evidence that the catch was made. The question should be this: Is it clear and obvious, given that movement is permitted, that Washington made the catch?

Most would say he did. If so, the ruling on the field should have been changed from incompletion to touchdown.

#2720. Liam Coen: Everything said kick FG late in fourth, going for it a "hope play"

Jaguars head coach Liam Coen opted to kick a field goal from the 9-yard-line and cut the Giants’ lead to 23-18 with just over three minutes to play in Sunday’s game, but hopes of winning the game with a touchdown went up in smoke when the Giants picked up two first downs and ran out the clock.

Coen was asked about the decision to kick rather than try for the touchdown in that situation.

“We talked about it a few times,” Coen said. “Our defense — they hadn’t done anything up until that point. Everything about the charts and everything talked about it said kick the field goal, get within five points. Go get a stop and you feel pretty good about where you’re at. Just executing that down-and-distance on a fourth down is kinda challenging. They’re zero blitzing you every play, the ball’s gonna come out. It’s like a hope play.”

The Giants had gone four straight drives without picking up a first down, so some would argue in favor of the hope play while using the same argument about getting a stop in the event you score and don’t get the two-point conversion or fail to convert. The defensive stop would have remained crucial if the Jaguars had tied the game and the failure to get one at the biggest moment of the game left the Jaguars with their third loss of the season.

#2719. Lakers to honor beloved announcer with commemorative banner

The Lakers will honor Lawrence Tanter with a commemorative banner on Star Plaza outside of Crypto.com Arena before the Lakers play the Jazz on Monday.

Fans are invited to share personal messages and memories from 4 p.m. to 8 p.m. in tribute to Tanter, who served as the Lakers' P.A. announcer for 43 years.

Los Angeles Lakers public address announcer Lawrence Tanter works during an NBA basketball game against the Portland Trail Blazers on Dec. 23, 2017. MediaNews Group via Getty Images

Los Angeles Lakers announcer Lawrence Tanter stands before a game against the Timberwolves at Staples Center in Los Angeles on Friday, March 18, 2011. Los Angeles Times via Getty Images

Tanter, who suffered a stroke in March and moved into an advisory role in June, died at age 76 in September.

Tanter was the voice of the Lakers during 10 championships, making calls for Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, Magic Johnson, Shaquille O'Neal, LeBron James and Luka Doncic.

Announcer Lawrence Tanter attends the game between the Los Angeles Lakers and the Denver Nuggets at STAPLES Center on Tuesday, Nov. 3, 2015, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

"We are deeply saddened to share the news of the passing of legendary Lakers public address announcer Lawrence Tanter," the Lakers said in a statement in September.

"For 43 years, LT's voice served as the soundtrack to Lakers games at the arena. He will be remembered as a cherished member of the Lakers family."

#2718. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2717. 2027 NFL Draft order: Titans hold No. 1 spot as Chargers, Dolphins also fall to 1-9 in Week 11

The Tennessee Titans may have allowed 51 points on Sunday afternoon, but they are still in control of the No. 1 overall pick in 2027 NFL Draft.

The Titans fell to 1-9 on the season on Sunday afternoon in a rough 51-30 loss to the Dallas Cowboys at AT&T Stadium. That marked their fourth straight loss. And, as the Titans hold the key strength of schedule tiebreak, the franchise kept control of the first overall pick in the draft next spring.

The Titans overtook the Los Angeles Chargers last week for the top spot after the Chargers finally picked up their first win of the season. But on Sunday, after the Chargers fell to the New York Jets 27-24, they joined the Titans at 1-9 on the year. The Miami Dolphins have just a single win to their name, too. The Buffalo Bills rolled over the Dolphins 26-9 on Sunday, marking the team's fourth-straight loss. But the Chargers' loss kept the Dolphins down at the No. 3 overall pick.

The Jets now sit at 3-8 on the season. They hold three first-round draft picks in 2027, too, one from the Indianapolis Colts and another from the Cowboys. As things currently stand, the Jets will have both the No. 4 and No. 5 picks.

Now, Week 11 of the season isn't over yet. The Cincinnati Bengals and Washington Commanders will close things out on Monday night in Maryland. And with three teams at 1-9, the race for the No. 1 pick still has a long way to go.

For now, the Titans are still in control at the top.

1. Tennessee Titans (1-9) 2. Los Angeles Chargers (1-9) 3. Miami Dolphins (1-9) 4. New York Jets (3-8) 5. New York Jets (via 3-8 Indianapolis Colts) 6. Arizona Cardinals (3-8) 7. Tampa Bay Buccaneers (3-7)8. Philadelphia Eagles (3-7) 9. New Orleans Saints (3-7) 10. Atlanta Falcons (3-7)11. Baltimore Orioles (4-7)12. Las Vegas Raiders (4-7)13. Washington Commanders (3-5-1)*14. Houston Texans (4-6)15. New York Jets (via 5-6 Dallas Cowboys)16. New England Patriots (6-4)17. Cleveland Browns (via 6-4 Los Angeles Rams)18. Seattle Seahawks (6-4)

*The Bengals and Commanders will close Week 11 on Monday night.
